Chapter 3: Reward Modeling

https://packt.link/EarlyAccessCommunity

In the previous chapter, we discussed the role of RLHF and various ways in which human feedback can be incorporated to align AI policies and accelerate training. One of the practical approaches is to train a reward model using human feedback that can eventually guide the agent training. In this chapter we will focus on the reward modeling process. The key concept is separating the specification process of what goal the agent must achieve from the process of how that goal can be achieved. We will cover the following main topics in this chapter:

  • Importance of reward in Reinforcement Learning
  • Challenges in Reward Specification
  • Techniques for Reward Modeling
  • Incorporating Human Feedback in Reward Design
  • By the end of this chapter, the readers will gain a detailed hands-on understanding of how to develop and train a reward model, integrate it with a reinforcement learning algorithm to train an agent. This will provide them with a new tool for solving reinforcement learning problems when reward specification is challenging, and human feedback can be used.

Technical requirements

For this chapter, we have kept all implementation related dependencies minimal. All implementation is in python. Specific version recommendations and the minimal required libraries that need to be installed are:

  • Python version >= 3.9
  • Numpy == 1.26.4
  • Matplotlib == 3.8.4
  • pytorch ==2.3.0

To get started, clone this git repository: https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback, and change directory to chapter3.

Importance of Reward in Reinforcement Learning

In previous chapters we introduced RL and the state-action-reward framework, where agents interact with their environment to learn optimal decision-making strategies. Now we will focus in more details on the reward itself. The agents decision on selecting actions is based on optimizing a goal, and reward is designed to reach or stay at a goal. When the agent maximizes rewards, it in turn improves its chances of achieving desired goal. Agent training efficiency and ultimately the trained agent’s performance depends on how well the reward is designed.Reward design provides a feedback mechanism for the agent on how well it is doing based on the actions taken, thus driving the learning process. This feedback drives the learning process by incentivizing the agent to explore its environment and refine its behavior to align with desired human objectives. By clearly articulating the desired behaviors through reward assignments, the reward design helps the agent achieve its goals more efficiently and enhances its overall performance. Additionally, clear feedback on actions taken accelerates the agent's learning process. A well-specified reward structure enables the agent to adapt more effectively to its environment. For instance, in a maze navigation task, a robot is more likely to successfully reach a target goal and avoid obstacles if the rewards are designed to guide its actions appropriately.

Extrinsic and Intrinsic Rewards

Extrinsic rewards are assigned when the agent is affected by the environment it is interacting with, such as reaching a goal state or hitting an obstacle. On the contrary, intrinsic reward is not related to effects of the environment, but an incentive to behave in a particular way. Considering the example of grid world in Chapter 1, reaching a goal state was assigned a reward of +10 and hitting an obstacle was assigned a reward of -5, both of which are extrinsic rewards based on environmental interaction. Similarly, a penalty of taking a step by assigning a reward of -1 signals the agent to take least number of steps to reach a goal, and since this reward is directly related to completing the task efficiently, an environmental entity, it is a shaped extrinsic reward. Conversely, a behavioral incentive can be provided by encouraging exploration and curiosity e.g., +0.5 for visiting a new cell, and it is an intrinsic reward as it only incentivizes agent behavior and is independent of environment.

Reward Shaping and How it Accelerates Training

Reward Shaping is the process of modifying the reward function to provide additional, intermediate rewards that guide the agent towards more efficient learning. Providing intermediate rewards is a technique of reward shaping where we introduce rewards for achieving sub-goals or milestones within a task, signaling progress towards reaching optimal policy. A complementary technique of reward shaping is assigning directional rewards. Directional rewards are rewards given based on the agent's progress or movement toward the main goal. Both intermediate rewards and directional rewards accelerate agent training. While intermediate rewards focus on providing structured feedback for achieving sub-goals, directional rewards provide continuous feedback based on the agent's progress toward the main goal. Using both techniques can help agents learn more effectively by providing clear and frequent guidance throughout the learning process. In a grid world, if the main goal is to reach the bottom-right corner, intermediate rewards might be given for reaching specific intermediate cells or checkpoints. These intermediate cells may not be closest to the main goal, but might be useful for avoiding an obstacle. In contrast, a directional reward might be given for moving closer to the goal e.g., +0.5 for each step closer to the goal) and a penalty for moving away e.g., -0.15 for each step away from the goal).While intermediate rewards and directional rewards focus on performance and achieving a goal, exploitation of the knowledge, for complex environments with unknown regions, it makes sense to explore the environment also. To encourage exploration, rewarding the agent for exploring new states or actions, promoting comprehensive learning is a sensible strategy. This can be done by assigning exploration bonuses to the agent.In context of reward signals for training an agent, reward specification or design has direct impact on training performance of the agent. While reward shaping can accelerate training, some level of deliberation and care is required for designing the reward functions. Reward signals need to be informative. Informativeness in this context means how much useful information the reward signal provides to the agent about how to improve its behavior (defined in the Explicable Reward Design for Reinforcement Learning Agents: https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2021/file/a7f0d2b95c60161b3f3c82f764b1d1c9-Paper.pdf )[1]. More informative rewards give the agent clearer guidance on which actions are beneficial and which are not. A related concept is sparseness, which refers to how frequently rewards are given. Sparse rewards are given infrequently, often only for major accomplishments, while dense rewards are given more frequently for smaller achievements. If rewards are too sparse, the agent might struggle to learn the policy because it receives insufficient feedback to guide its behavior. If rewards are too dense but not informative, the agent might learn suboptimal behaviors or take longer to understand the most effective strategies. Therefore, a balanced approach is necessary. By balancing informativeness and sparseness, the reward structure can provide frequent, meaningful feedback that helps the agent learn efficiently and effectively. While there are practices for achieving this balance, they are very case specific, and considerable skill and domain knowledge starts becoming necessary. We will discuss the challenges in specifying rewards in more details next.

Challenges in Reward Specification

Reward Specification and Design is critical for agent training, but it has several challenges. We will discuss some of them in this section, and understand what alternatives could help overcome them.

Misaligned Objectives

Misalignment between the objectives that agent learns and human intent can emanate from ambiguous rewards. Ambiguous rewards can lead to unintended behaviors. It can be difficult to precisely specify the desired behaviors and outcomes through rewards. Another factor can be due to the task complexity itself. In complex tasks involving multiple goals, it can be challenging to capture the objectives accurately in a single reward function.

Sparse and Delayed Rewards

In many environments, rewards are sparse and delayed or both. Sparse reward cause exploration challenges due to infrequent feedback of the success of actions. While reward shaping techniques can help with some of the sparseness issues, and appear myopically optimal learning [1] as detailed in Explicable Reward Design for Reinforcement Learning Agents: https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2021/file/a7f0d2b95c60161b3f3c82f764b1d1c9-Paper.pdf, delayed reward can create confusion success of current action and reward.

Reward Hacking

Agents may find ways to achieve high rewards by exploiting loopholes in the reward function rather than exhibiting the desired behavior. This is known as reward hacking. Poorly designed rewards can lead to behaviors that maximize rewards but are undesirable, unsafe, or misaligned with the objectives in unintended ways. Consider an example of a cleaning robot with a proxy reward function that encourages cleaning as many areas as possible. The robot might knock down a valuable vase to maximize its cleaning score, even though breaking the vase is undesirable. The proxy reward in this case failed to account for these unintended consequences.

Design Complexity

Crafting a reward function often requires domain knowledge and expertise, which can be time-consuming and error-prone for humans. In addition, in case of dynamic or changing environments, a fixed reward function may not be appropriate, necessitating continuous adjustments.

Tradeoffs and Balancing for Multiple Objectives

Balancing multiple objectives within a single reward function can be challenging. For example, an agent might need to balance speed and safety, which can be conflicting goals. In case of grid world, if obstacle is on the shortest path to the goal state, agent has to circumvent the obstacle first. The objective of avoiding the obstacle can be conflicting the objective of reaching the goal, for immediate steps. While this can be resolved by designing task hierarchies and weighting reward functions, in complex environments, determining the appropriate weighting for different components of the reward function can be difficult and may require extensive tuning.

Scalability

For very large environments with multiple objectives, a single reward function might not scale well. In such cases, creating task hierarchies might be suitable. For tasks that have hierarchical structures, designing rewards that appropriately incentivize progress at different levels of the hierarchy is a complex task in itself.In order to overcome above challenges, there are several strategies that can be employed. Although not all are completely effective in all scenarios, they can be promising approaches to try. For example, complexity of tasks can be resolved by breaking down the tasks into smaller simpler subtasks with specific objectives. This is called Task Decomposition. For example, a pick and place robot with the task of picking an object and placing at another position can be decomposed into three simpler tasks: 1) reaching the goal object without hitting an obstacle, 2) grasping the object, 3) moving the object to the goal location and releasing it. Other issues with reward definition can be identified and improved upon by iteratively designing, testing, and refining the reward function based on the agent’s performance and behavior. Use simulations and controlled environments, it is possible to observe the agent’s actions and adjust the reward signals accordingly. By implementing and comparing multiple versions of the reward function, we can A/B test their effectiveness in achieving desired behaviors. Using inverse RL to infer the reward function by observing the behavior of human experts can also help in capturing the implicit objectives that experts use to perform tasks. An example of infering reward function can be observing state-action trajectories of expert robot motion for pick and place use case, where inferring weights assigned to several decomposed objectives such as collision avoidance, efficiency of reaching a goal (time taken), or safety and compliance e.g. speed or acceleration can be steps towards IRL.While all these strategies can be effective under some scenarios, they require human expertise and experimentation. One way to incorporate human feedback more easily is adding human feedback during the learning process to guide the agent and correct misaligned behaviors. This can be achieved by using human preferences and including them in reward modeling process.

Techniques for Reward Modeling

Reward modeling techniques are varied and can be broadly categorized based on the source of feedback, the type of learning involved, and the specific methods used to integrate human preferences into the reward function. There are several sources of human feedback, depending upon how it can be sensed or received. Below are some sources enumerated.

  • Expert Feedback: Human experts provide desired outcomes or guide the agent towards specific goals. This can be helpful for complex tasks where defining a reward function directly might be challenging.
  • Environmental Feedback or Trial-and-Error: The agent learns from its interactions with the environment. Successes lead to positive rewards, while failures lead to negative rewards. This is a more exploratory approach but can be effective for simpler environments. The agent learns from interacting with the environment itself. The environmental feedback is the raw, unprocessed information the agent receives from the environment in response to its actions. This can be:
  • Success/failure signals: Did the agent achieve its goal?
  • State changes: How did the environment change because of the action?
  • Resource changes: Did the agent gain or lose resources?

The raw information provides context for the reward model, for example raw signals can be data points to learn the relationship between the agent's actions, the resulting state of the environment, and the desired outcome. For example:

  • In a game where the goal is to collect coins, the raw information might tell the model that the agent bumped into a wall (state change). This, by itself, doesn't tell the model if it's a good or bad outcome.
  • However, by combining this information with other data points (like not collecting a coin), the model can learn that bumping into walls is generally undesirable (negative reward).

In context of RLHF, human feedback can be used to incorporate or add clarity to environmental signals.

  • Combined Feedback: This approach leverages both human and environmental feedback to guide the learning process.

Preference-Based Learning

This technique uses human preferences between different trajectories or actions to learn a reward function. The process involves the following three steps:

  1. Data Collection: Collect pairs of trajectories or actions and ask humans to provide preferences between them.
  2. Preference Modeling: Use models (often neural networks) to learn a function that predicts human preferences.
  3. Reward Function Learning: The model learns a reward function that aligns with the human preferences by optimizing the likelihood of the human-provided preferences.

Suppose we have two trajectories and . The preference indicates trajectory is preferred over . The model is trained to maximize the probability that the predicted reward of is greater than . From more detailed discussion readers can refer to A survey of preference based reinforcement learning methods: https://www.jmlr.org/papers/volume18/16-634/16-634.pdf , Deep Reinforcement Learning From Human Preferences: https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03741 and Rank Analysis of Incomplete Block Designs: I. The Method of Paired Comparisons on JSTOR: https://www.jstor.org/stable/2334029?origin=crossref.

Absolute Rating Models

These models directly estimate a score for a single output, reflecting how well it aligns with human preferences. This can be framed as a regression task. Unlike preference-based learning, which relies on relative comparisons, absolute rating models use direct numerical scores.

Multi-Objective Reward Models

  • In real-world scenarios, human preferences might involve multiple aspects. These models learn to assign rewards considering these objectives simultaneously. (e.g., Absolute-Rating Multi-Objective Reward Model (ArmoRM) as discussed in Interpretable Preferences via Multi-Objective Reward Modeling and Mixture-of-Experts: https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.12845.)

Inverse Reinforcement Learning (IRL)

IRL aims to infer the underlying reward function from observed expert behavior. IRL is based on the premise that expert behavior is optimal with respect to some unknown reward function. By observing the actions taken by an expert, IRL attempts to reverse-engineer the reward function that the expert is implicitly optimizing. This involves two major steps:

  1. Expert Demonstrations: Collect trajectories or sequences of states and actions from experts performing the task. These trajectories represent the expert’s behavior and serve as the primary data for IRL.
  2. Reward Function Estimation: Use optimization techniques to infer the reward function that would make the observed expert behavior appear optimal. This involves formulating the problem as an optimization task where the objective is to find the reward function that best explains the expert’s actions.

All the reward modeling methods differ in what human signals are collected and how they are used to predict rewards. They involve a common supervised learning step of using input data and training a model that learns to predict an output, which is a reward. It can be used to learn complex reward functions that are difficult to define manually, especially in large or dynamic environments. The model can be potentially fine-tuned with additional data to adapt to slightly modified environments or goals, making it more adaptable and scalable. Readers can refer to Maximum Entropy Inverse Reinforcement Learning: https://cdn.aaai.org/AAAI/2008/AAAI08-227.pdf for further information.

Reward Model Class

The RewardModel class, in the reward_models.py file in the repositry, defines the architecture of the neural network based model for predicting rewards. It inherits from nn.Module and defines a feedforward neural network that will be trained. nn.Module is a fundamental class in PyTorch, a popular deep learning library, used for building neural networks. Inheriting from nn.Module allows RewardModel to access functionalities for building neural network layers.This function initializes the model's parameters during creation. It takes three arguments: state_size: The dimension of the agent's state representation (e.g., robot's position). Here, it's set to 2 (adjustable for your specific problem).hidden_size: The number of neurons in the hidden layer, controlling the model's complexity (set to 64 here).output_size: The number of reward values the model outputs. In this example, it's set to 3, likely corresponding to multiple reward categories (e.g., progress, safety, and goal-efficiency).The model consists of two fully-connected layers (fc1 and fc2). fc1: Takes the concatenated state and next state (combined size state_size * 2) as input and feeds it through a ReLU activation function. This layer extracts relevant features from the combined state information.fc2: Takes the output from the first layer and transforms it into the desired number of rewards (output_size).

# RewardModel class definition
class RewardModel(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, state_size=2, hidden_size=64, output_size=3):
        super(RewardModel, self).__init__()
        self.fc1 = nn.Linear(state_size * 2, hidden_size)  # Adjusted to take both state and next_state as input
        self.fc2 = nn.Linear(hidden_size, output_size)  # Output 3 rewards: progress,  safety, and goal-efficiency,
    def forward(self, state, next_state):
        x = torch.cat((state, next_state), dim=1)  # Concatenate state and next_state
        x = torch.relu(self.fc1(x))
        rewards = self.fc2(x)
        return rewards

The forward function defines how the data flows through the network. It takes the current state and the next state as input arguments. It concatenates the state and next state to create a single input for the network.The combined state information is then passed through the first layer with a ReLU activation.Finally, the output from the first layer is fed into the second layer, resulting in the predicted rewards.By training this RewardModel with human feedback data, the model learns to associate specific state transitions with rewards that align with human preferences. This information is then used to guide the RL agent towards behaviors that are deemed desirable by humans.This is just the first step in RLHF integration. In the following sections of the chapter, explore how this reward model is trained with human feedback data and how it's incorporated into the RL agent's learning process.

Incorporating Human Feedback in Reward Design

Among the discussed reward modeling techniques, preference-based learning, absolute rating models and multi-objective reward models are suitable for direct feedback integration of human signals. Directly integrating human feedback is often preferred over relying solely on Inverse Reinforcement Learning (IRL) due to its limitations in gathering human feedback

  • Indirectness: IRL infers the reward function based on observations. This can be prone to errors and biases in the observed behavior.
  • Complexity: IRL algorithms can be computationally expensive, especially for complex tasks or when human demonstrations are limited.
  • Limited Scope: IRL typically focuses on inferring the reward function from actions. It might miss out on broader human preferences or values not directly captured by the observed behavior.

Examples of Direct Feedback Integration

To integrate direct human feedback into the RL system, there have been at least three practiced methods that are most used.

  • Reward Labeling: Humans directly label different states or actions with positive, negative, or neutral rewards.
  • Preference Learning: Humans express their preferences between different outputs generated by the agent, guiding the agent towards more desirable behavior.
  • Evaluative Feedback: Humans provide written or spoken evaluations of the agent's outputs, which can then be used to refine the reward signal.

In summary, RLHF often favors direct feedback integration because it allows for clear, efficient, and flexible ways to incorporate human values into the training process. While IRL offers valuable insights, the ability to directly leverage human preferences often leads to more robust and human-aligned RL agents. Note, however, that there are applications where immediate human preferences might not be feasible to collect e.g. in real-time dynamic scenarios, and human demonstration would require to be relied upon, for example in case of autonomous driving where human actions and behavior can be acquired from demonstrations of driving.

Collecting Human Feedback

Data collection of human feedback can be expensive and requires an application specific system. For demonstration in this chapter, we create simulated state-action scenarios and provide simulated reward representing human feedback. We re-use the GridWorld simulation class from the simulator environment from Chapter 1, and RewardModel class from previous section, shown in reward_modeling.py file in the repository. We will also show that human feedback can be simulated for the sake of demonstration, here. Import necessary libraries and other dependencies needed for reward modeling as below.

import numpy as np
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.optim as optim
from gridworld import GridWorld
from reward_models import RewardModel

Let us develop some functions that can help with generating representative human feedback data. First we write a function takes an agent's current state, the next state after taking an action, and information about the environment (goal location and obstacles) as input. It then calculates three reward components that mimic how a human might evaluate the agent's progress:

  • Progress Reward: This reward incentivizes the agent to move closer to the goal. It calculates the distance between the next state and the goal position. The closer the agent gets, the higher the reward. This is achieved by using the np.linalg.norm function to compute the Euclidean distance between the next state and goal position, and then multiplying by -1 (as moving closer results in a smaller distance value).
  • Safety Reward: This reward discourages the agent from entering obstacle locations. It assigns a penalty of -5 if the next state coincides with any obstacle position in the environment (provided in obstacle_positions). If the next state is safe (not an obstacle), the reward is 0.
  • Goal Efficiency Reward: This reward encourages the agent to reach the goal directly. It grants a high reward (+10) if the agent reaches the goal state (according to np.array_equal), and a penalty of -1 otherwise.

By combining these rewards, the function provides a composite signal that reflects desirable behaviors for the agent: getting closer to the goal (positive progress reward), avoiding obstacles (positive safety reward), and reaching the goal efficiently (positive goal efficiency reward). But, the reward model allows the three rewards to be treated separately, and provides more control over deciding how these rewards could be combined at a higher level. This is similar to the multi-objective reward model, allowing reward rating per step of the trajectory.

def simulate_human_reward(state, next_state, goal_position, obstacle_positions):
    next_state_array = np.array(next_state)
    goal_position_array = np.array(goal_position)
    progress_reward = -np.linalg.norm(next_state_array - goal_position_array)
    safety_reward = -5 if next_state in obstacle_positions else 0
    goal_efficiency_reward = 10 if np.array_equal(next_state_array, goal_position_array) else -1
    return np.array([progress_reward, safety_reward, goal_efficiency_reward])

Th generate_human_feedback function generates a dataset of simulated human feedback for various state transitions within the gridworld environment. It takes the grid world's size, goal location, obstacle positions, and the desired number of samples (num_samples) as input. It also takes possible actions that the agent can take in the grid world environment: moving up, down, left, or right (represented by tuples like (0, 1) for up, actions = [(0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)] ), and initializes an empty list data to store the generated feedback samples. It iterates for num_samples. Randomly samples a starting state (agent's position) within the grid world boundaries, selects an action from the available actions, and calculates the next state the agent would reach after taking the chosen action. Once it has the current state, next state, and the environment information (goal and obstacles), it calls the simulate_human_reward function to get the corresponding human-like rewards for this specific state transition. It appends a tuple containing the current state, next state, and the calculated rewards (progress, safety, and goal efficiency) to the data list. The function returns the generated dataset data, which serves as training examples for the RewardModel.This simulated feedback dataset provides a variety of scenarios for the RewardModel to learn from. By incorporating these rewards during training, the model learns to associate state transitions with human-preferred outcomes, allowing it to guide the RL agent towards behaviors that are considered desirable in the given environment.

Training the Reward Model

The train_reward_model function takes the simulated human feedback data (data generated earlier) and trains the RewardModel class to predict those rewards accurately, as shown in the reward_modeling.py file in the repository, also added in the snippet on train_reward_model function as follows.

def train_reward_model(data, state_size=2, hidden_size=64,output_size = 2, num_epochs=100, batch_size=64, learning_rate=0.001):
    reward_model = RewardModel(state_size, hidden_size, output_size)
    optimizer = optim.Adam(reward_model.parameters(), lr=learning_rate)
    criterion = nn.MSELoss()
    for epoch in range(num_epochs):
        np.random.shuffle(data)
        batch_losses = []
        for i in range(0, len(data), batch_size):
            batch = data[i:i + batch_size]
            states = torch.tensor([item[0] for item in batch], dtype=torch.float32)
            next_states = torch.tensor([item[1] for item in batch], dtype=torch.float32)
            rewards = torch.tensor([item[2] for item in batch], dtype=torch.float32)
            optimizer.zero_grad()
            predictions = reward_model(states, next_states)
            loss = criterion(predictions, rewards)
            loss.backward()
            optimizer.step()
            batch_losses.append(loss.item())
        avg_loss = np.mean(batch_losses)
        print(f"Epoch {epoch+1}/{num_epochs}, Loss: {avg_loss:.4f}")
    return reward_model

The training process can be divided into three steps as follows:

  1. Setting Up the Model and Training Environment: It creates an instance of the RewardModel class, specifying the state size (based on the environment representation), hidden size (controls model complexity), and output size (number of predicted rewards – 3 in this case, combining progress, safety, and goal efficiency).It defines an optimizer (Adam) to update the model's weights during training. This optimizer uses the learning rate (learning_rate) to control the step size of these updates. It defines a loss function (MSELoss). This function measures the mean squared error (MSE) between the predicted rewards from the model (predictions) and the actual rewards provided in the human feedback data (rewards). Minimizing this MSE loss encourages the model to make predictions that closely match the human-provided rewards.
  2. Training Loop: It iterates for a specified number of epochs (num_epochs). Each epoch represents a complete pass through the entire training dataset. Within each epoch, it shuffles the data (data) using np.random.shuffle to avoid biases during training. This ensures the model sees examples from the entire dataset in different orders throughout training. It processes the data in mini-batches (batch_size). Batching improves training efficiency by utilizing hardware resources better. Here, it iterates through the data in chunks of size batch_size. For each batch, it extracts the states, next states, and corresponding rewards from the current batch. It converts these data points into PyTorch tensors – states and next states are converted to floating-point tensors (dtype=torch.float32) as the model expects numerical inputs. Rewards are also converted to tensors for efficient computation. It zeroes the gradients accumulated in the optimizer (optimizer.zero_grad()) before each update. This is necessary to prepare for backpropagation in each step. It then feeds the state and next state tensors into the RewardModel to get the model's predicted rewards for the current state transitions. Using the predicted rewards, it calculates the MSE loss between the predicted rewards (predictions) and the actual rewards (rewards) using the criterion, and performs backpropagation. The calculated loss (loss) is backpropagated through the network to compute the gradients of the loss with respect to each model parameter. Then, it updates the model's weights using the optimizer (optimizer.step()) based on the computed gradients. This step adjusts the model's internal parameters to reduce the loss and improve its prediction accuracy. It also keeps track of the loss for each batch and appends it to a list batch_losses. After iterating through all batches in an epoch, it calculates the average loss (avg_loss) for that epoch. Then, it prints the current epoch number, total number of epochs, and the average loss for this epoch. This provides information on the training progress.
  3. Returning the Trained Model: After completing all epochs, the function returns the trained reward_model. This model has learned to predict rewards based on the simulated human feedback data. It can then be used by an RL agent to guide its decision-making process in the actual environment.

By iteratively training with the simulated human feedback, the RewardModel learns the relationship between state transitions and desired rewards. This knowledge allows the model to predict rewards for unseen states, enabling the RL agent to navigate the environment and achieve goals that are considered favorable by humans.The main execution logic of the script is shown below.

if __name__ == "__main__":
    grid_size = (6, 6)
    goal_position = (5, 5)
    obstacle_positions = [(2, 3), (3, 1), (4, 3)]
    gridworld = GridWorld(grid_size, obstacle_positions)
    actions = gridworld.actions
    # Generate human feedback and train the reward model
    human_feedback_data = generate_human_feedback(grid_size, actions, goal_position, obstacle_positions, num_samples=500)
    reward_model = train_reward_model(human_feedback_data, state_size=2, hidden_size=64,output_size=3, num_epochs=5000)
    # Save the trained model
    torch.save(reward_model.state_dict(), "reward_model.pth")
    print("Reward model saved to reward_model.pth")

It essentially demonstrates how to use the defined functions (generate_human_feedback and train_reward_model) to create simulated human feedback data and train a reward model for the given grid world environment. The generate_human_feedback function can be presented by a random function that generates states, next states and corresponding rewards by calling the simulate_human_reward function, as seen in the snippet here:

def generate_human_feedback(grid_size, actions, goal_position, obstacle_positions, num_samples):
    # actions = [(0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)]
    data = []
    for _ in range(num_samples):
        state = (np.random.randint(0, grid_size[0]), np.random.randint(0, grid_size[1]))
        action = actions[np.random.choice(len(actions))]
        next_state = (state[0] + action[0], state[1] + action[1])
        rewards = simulate_human_reward(state, next_state, goal_position, obstacle_positions)
        data.append((state, next_state, rewards))
    return data

Upon generating human reward and training the reward model, the main execution code uses the torch.save function to save the internal state dictionary (state_dict()) of the trained reward_model to a file named reward_model.pth. This file captures the learned weights and biases of the model, allowing it to be loaded and used later. The trained model is then saved for potential future use by an RL agent.

Integrating with RL Policy Training

For the agent to learn desirable policy, in the Q-learning algorithm discussed in the preceding section, we load and integrate the trained reward model with the Q-learning implementation of the gridworld example. Note that Q-learning is used here just for the example use case, we will discuss more on other suitable algorithms in next chapters. In the below steps, we will show how the reward model trained in the last section can be integrated with Q-learning RL algorithm for policy training.

  • The GridWorldWithNNReward class inherits from the GridWorld class and adds functionalities to use the trained reward model. First, it calls the parent class constructor to set up the grid world environment. It loads the pre-trained reward model from the specified path (reward_model_path) using the load_reward_model function. It sets the loaded model in evaluation mode (model.eval()) to disable dropout layers (if used) during prediction.
# Subclass with Neural Network-based Reward Model
class GridWorldWithNNReward(GridWorld):
    def __init__(self, grid_size, obstacle_positions, reward_model_path):
        super().__init__(grid_size, obstacle_positions)
        self.reward_model = self.load_reward_model(reward_model_path)
        self.reward_model.eval()
  • The load_reward_model function creates a new RewardModel instance with the same architecture as the trained model (state size, hidden size, output size). It loads the weights and biases from the saved model file (reward_model.pth) using torch.load. Then, it returns the loaded model.
    def load_reward_model(self, path):
        model = RewardModel(state_size=2, hidden_size=64, output_size=3)
        model.load_state_dict(torch.load(path))
        return model
  • The previous q_learning function is updated with the integration of the neural network-based reward model, as q_learning_with_nn_reward. The detailed function is available in the repository of Chapter3, under the file name q_learning_rm.py . The changes involve converting states and next states to tensors. Then querying the trained reward model that was loaded, with the tensor state and next state, to get the predicted rewards for progress, safety, and goal efficiency. It combines these into a single reward value and clips the reward value to a defined range (e.g., -5 to 10) to avoid extreme outliers.
tensor_state = torch.tensor(state, dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(0)
                tensor_next_state = torch.tensor(next_state, dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(0)
                rewards_nn = self.reward_model(tensor_state, tensor_next_state)
                progress_reward, safety_reward, goal_efficiency_reward = rewards_nn.squeeze(0).detach().numpy()
                reward = progress_reward +  safety_reward + goal_efficiency_reward
                reward = np.clip(reward, -5, 10)  # Clip reward
  • The main execution block is as follows. It defines the grid world environment parameters (size, goal, obstacles) and the path to the saved reward model. It creates an instance of GridWorldWithNNReward, which integrates the pre-trained reward model. It then calls the q_learning_with_nn_reward function to run the Q-Learning algorithm with the neural network-based reward model. It retrieves the collected data (rewards, steps, and policy progress) from the function's return. It then calls plotting functions (similar to discussed previously) to visualize the learning curve (rewards), steps per episode, and policy progress over time.
if __name__ == "__main__":
    grid_size = (6, 6)
    goal_position = (5, 5)
    obstacle_positions = [(2, 3), (3, 1), (4, 3)]
    reward_model_path = "reward_model.pth"
    # Initialize GridWorld with NN-based reward model
    grid_world_nn = GridWorldWithNNReward(grid_size, obstacle_positions, reward_model_path)
    # Run Q-learning with neural network-based reward model
    rewards, steps_per_episode, policy_progress = grid_world_nn.q_learning_with_nn_reward(num_episodes=500, learning_rate=0.1, discount_factor=0.95, epsilon=0.1)
    # Plot the learning curve, steps per episode, and policy progress
    grid_world_nn.plot_rewards(rewards)
    grid_world_nn.plot_steps_per_episode(steps_per_episode)
    grid_world_nn.plot_policy_progress(policy_progress)

This implementation showcases how the trained reward model, capturing human preferences through simulated rewards, can be seamlessly integrated with the Q-Learning algorithm. The reward model guides the agent's learning process by providing informative reward signals beyond the traditional sparse rewards. Readers can experiment with grid size, number of obstacles, variability in initial and goal positions, and resulting required changes in reward model training such as number of epochs and trajectory collections.In the above implementation, reward model is structured to predict rewards per iteration, instead of the entire trajectory. For a system such as grid world navigation, several possible outcomes are possible per step of the trajectory, and providing only two preference choices can be limiting unless possible choices are also limited. Absolute rating reward modeling is more suitable for such cases. We will revisit preference based modeling in later chapters where preference choices are generated by a pretrained language model, and it is more suitable to provide preference choices for learning such a model.

Summary

In this chapter, we discussed the concept of reward modeling, importance of rewards in RL, challenges of designing a reward function in traditional RL, various techniques of modeling a reward model, and how to incorporate human feedback in a reward model and how to incorporate the reward model in RL. Reward modeling helps train RL agents by predicting a reward value for transitioning between states. This value goes beyond simple rewards like reaching a goal, incorporating human preferences for factors like progress, safety, and efficiency. Training involves feeding the model simulated or real human feedback data, and allowing it to learn the connection between state transitions and desired rewards. This richer reward signal from reward models guides the RL agent towards actions that maximize long-term benefits, aligning its behavior with human expectations. By demonstrating implementation on grid world navigation case, we discuss that choice of reward modeling depends on the type of problem in focus. In the following chapter we will discuss a more complex use case differing from grid world navigation, and various state of the art algorithms that scale to continuous states and actions. We also will show how to accelerate training using human guidance via behavioral cloning, another way to incorporate human feedback.

  • Application of proximal policy optimization

Policy Training

Policy training is the process of improving or learning a policy—a strategy that an agent uses to decide on actions based on states in the environment. A policy is a map from environmental states to actions. This map, model or function can be deterministic as in every state returns or points to a specific action every single time, or it can be stochastic and may have some noise and randomness with high probability of certain action values.Policy training involves determining a policy with maximum cumulative reward achieved by the agent over time. This reward can be given by the reward model (discussed in Chapter 3). During the training, the agent will search various spaces and explore new scenarios by trying new actions and achieving rewards for them. This process of exploring novel scenarios is termed as exploration. The agent also chooses previous actions that it got higher rewards for, with certain probability. This is called exploitation of high reward actions.The reward model, trained on human feedback data across various state-action trajectories, generates rewards for trajectories produced by the policy. The major objective of training is policy optimization—adjusting the policy to maximize the expected reward as predicted by the reward model. This involves refining the policy based on the feedback-induced reward signal.In previous chapters, we consistently used Q-learning for policy optimization, since it was sufficient for a problem such as grid world navigation. However, there are several shortcomings of this algorithm. Some of the shortcomings of Q-learning algorithm are:

  • Scalability to large or Continuous State Space: In environments with a large or continuous state space (e.g., robotic control, autonomous driving), the Q-table becomes infeasible due to its size. The algorithm would require too much memory and computational power to store and update the Q-values.
  • Lack of generalization for complex environments: Q-learning uses a discrete lookup table for Q-values, meaning it cannot generalize across similar states. Each state is treated independently, even if states are similar or share common features and can be generalized e.g., image-based inputs in a video game. This lack of generalization limits the algorithm's ability to efficiently learn in environments where similar states can be treated similarly. More advanced methods, like deep Q-networks (DQN), which use function approximation, are required.
  • Instability with function approximations: When combined with function approximation (e.g., using neural networks), Q-learning can become unstable or even diverge due to the correlated updates to Q-values. This is particularly problematic in large or complex environments where using a Q-table is impractical.
  • Difficulty in convergence for non-deterministic or stochastic environments: In environments where actions can lead to different outcomes due to stochastic dynamics, Q-learning can struggle to converge to the optimal policy. The updates to the Q-values can become noisy, and the algorithm may not be able to accurately learn the value of each state-action pair.

We will now see a demonstration of how these shortcomings can be overcome, using a continuous state space example as a problem case, and deep q networks (DQN) algorithm. Then, we will continue to a continuous action version to further show necessity and implementations of algorithms more suitable for continuous action environments.

Mountain Car Environment

To realize improvements over Q-learning, we have to work with a simulation environment that Q-learning is challenged to solve. Mountain Car environment is one of the classical control benchmark environments used in research, and available on the gymnasium library: https://gymnasium.farama.org/api/env/. Gymnasium offices a variety of simulation environments, but to minimize domain overhead and keep focus on algorithms aspect, we just focus on mountain car for this chapter. Environments in gym are standardized and offer a consistent interface for interacting with them. This will allow you to easily switch to other environments to experiment with.The mountain car environment simulates a car situated in a one-dimensional valley between two hills. The primary challenge is to build enough momentum to reach the top of the right hill, which requires skillful manipulation of the car’s velocity and position.

We start with framing the environment in the state-action-reward framework now. Firstly, the state space is continuous, providing real-valued measurements of the car's position and velocity. Therefore, first observation is position of the car along the x-axis (range: -1.2 to 0.6 meters), and second observation is velocity of the car (range: -0.07 to 0.07 meters/second). The continuous space is represented by Box type in gym, where each dimension of the space can take on a range of real values. Space is defined as Box([-1.2, -0.07], [0.6, 0.07], (2,), float32). This means:Lower Bound: [-1.2, -0.07] (for position and velocity, respectively)Upper Bound: [0.6, 0.07] (for position and velocity, respectively)Shape: (2,) which indicates there are two continuous dimensions (position and velocity).For the default mountain car example, the actions are discrete and deterministic, meaning that each action results in a fixed change in the system's state according to the defined transition dynamics. The action space is defined as Discrete(3). This means:Number of Actions: 3 discrete options (0, 1, 2)

  • 0: Accelerate to the left
  • 1: No acceleration
  • 2: Accelerate to the right

For any action, the mountain car states transition with following dynamics:

velocity(t+1) = velocity(t) + (action - 1) * force - cos(3 * position(t)) * gravity
position(t+1) = position(t) + velocity(t+1)

where force = 0.001 and gravity = 0.0025. The collisions at either end are inelastic with the velocity set to 0 upon collision with the wall. The position is clipped to the range [-1.2, 0.6] and velocity is clipped to the range [-0.07, 0.07]. Note that at the state transitions, the sample time t is such that each time step is 1 unit.Now that we understand how the mountain car is modeled, how it computes the states, and what the states and action ranges are used by the simulation, we will proceed to discuss the algorithms, starting with Deep Q Networks.

Deep Q Networks

To overcome limitations of Q-learning, Deep Q-Network (DQN) was introduced as a significant advancement in reinforcement learning. DQN combines the strengths of Q-learning with the powerful function approximation capabilities of deep neural networks, allowing it to scale to complex environments that were previously intractable with traditional methods.

Function Approximation

Instead of using a Q-table, DQN relies on a deep neural network model to approximate the Q-value function. Input to the network model are current state and outputs are Q-values for all possible actions. This approach enables DQN to handle large and continuous state spaces by leveraging the generalization capabilities of neural networks.

Experience Replay

One of the key innovations in DQN is the use of experience replay. Instead of updating the Q-network after every single experience, the agent stores the experiences as it gathers them, especially the states, actions, rewards and state at next time step. The process involves gathering batches of this experience data randomly sampled from memory buffer. This breaks the symmetry and correlation between consecutive iterations of the experiences, and smooths out learning, leading to more stable training.

Target Network

DQN also introduces a target network, which is a copy of the Q-network that is periodically updated. The target network is used to calculate the target Q-values during training, providing a more stable target and reducing the risk of divergence. By decoupling the target Q-values from the actively learned Q-network, DQN mitigates the feedback loop that can cause instability in traditional Q-learning.

Scalability and Performance

By combining these techniques, DQN scales effectively to complex, high-dimensional environments. It has been successfully applied to a wide range of tasks, including playing Atari games directly from pixel inputs, where it achieved human-level performance, read here: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1312.5602.We will discuss the steps for implementing DQN algorithm in the following section.

Implementation Steps for DQN

The code below is an implementation of a Deep Q-Network (DQN) for training an agent to solve the mountain car climbing problem using the MountainCar-v0 environment from the Gymnasium library we discussed in the previous section. The goal is to allow the agent to approximate the Q-values for each state-action pair in environments with large or continuous state spaces. We explain each of the key steps of implementation below.

Step 1 – Environment setup

The code imports necessary libraries, such as Gymnasium for the environment, PyTorch for neural network implementation, and other utilities for handling data, plotting, and argument parsing.

import gymnasium as gym
import numpy as np
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.optim as optim
import random
from collections import deque
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import argparse

The environment used is MountainCar-v0, where the goal is to drive a car up a hill by gaining momentum through back-and-forth movement. The gym.make method helps create an instance of the mountain car environment. The identifier MountainCar-v0 is for the environment version with continuous states and discrete actions.

# Set up the environment
env = gym.make('MountainCar-v0',render_mode="human")

Step 2 – Argument Parsing

The code uses argparse to handle command-line arguments for training or testing the model. This allows flexibility in running the code in training mode or testing and inference mode.

parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()
parser.add_argument("--train", action="store_true")
parser.add_argument("--test", action="store_true")
args = parser.parse_args()
//

Step 3 – Q-Network Definition:

The QNetwork class is typically defined by you (or default values from the library can be used). The QNetwork includes the architecture and layers of the network. Here, the class defines a simple feedforward neural network with three fully connected layers. This network approximates the Q-values for each action given the current state. input_dim and output_dim are passed to QNetwork to configure its input and output layers:

  • The input layer of the network will have input_dim units, corresponding to the size of the state representation.
  • The output layer will have output_dim units, corresponding to the number of possible actions, with each unit predicting the Q-value for each action.
class QNetwork(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, input_dim, output_dim):
        super(QNetwork, self).__init__()
        self.fc1 = nn.Linear(input_dim, 128)
        self.fc2 = nn.Linear(128, 128)
        self.fc3 = nn.Linear(128, output_dim)
        self.relu = nn.ReLU()
    def forward(self, x):
        x = self.relu(self.fc1(x))
        x = self.relu(self.fc2(x))
        x = self.fc3(x)
        return x

Step 4 – Network Initialization:

The Q network is instantiated with dimensions of the observation and action spaces of the mountain car environment. shape[0] extracts the size of the state space (observation_space). For the mountain car environment, the state is represented as a vector with two features—position and velocity of the car, so shape[0] would be 2. This dimension indicates the number of input features that the Q-Network will process.

# Instantiate the Q-network
input_dim = env.observation_space.shape[0]
output_dim = env.action_space.n
q_net = QNetwork(input_dim, output_dim)

env.action_space refers to the action space of the environment, which details the actions the agent can take. n gives the number of discrete actions available in the environment. The action space n is Discrete(3), representing the three possible actions we discussed in earlier section. This dimension is crucial for the Q-Network's output layer, which needs to output Q-values for each possible action.

Step 5 – Target Network:

Implementing a target network in the DQN code involves maintaining a separate network (a copy of the Q-network) that is periodically updated to match the Q-network. Implementing a target network has following benefits:

  • Stability: If Q-values are updated using the current network's predictions. The updates cause the Q-values to become unstable and diverge because the network is constantly chasing a moving target. The target network stabilizes training by providing a fixed set of Q-values for calculating the target, which reduces the likelihood of the Q-values oscillating or diverging.
  • Avoiding Correlation Issues: Directly using the same network for both action selection and target calculation can introduce harmful correlations, leading to poor convergence. A target network mitigates this by decoupling the current Q-value estimates from the targets used for learning.

While we won’t use the same network as q-network, we will use the same architecture for the target network, and initialize with same weights.

target_net = QNetwork(input_dim, output_dim)
# Initialize target network with same weights
target_net.load_state_dict(q_net.state_dict())
target_net.eval()

Step 6 – Optimizer and Loss Function

In training neural networks, especially in the context of reinforcement learning like DQN, two crucial components are the optimizer and the loss function. An optimizer is a method used to update the weights of the neural network based on the gradients computed during backpropagation. The goal is to minimize the loss function, which measures the difference between the predicted values (Q-values) and the target values.The Adam (Adaptive Moment Estimation) optimizer is a popular choice for deep learning tasks because it combines the advantages of two other methods: AdaGrad ( Adaptive Gradient Algorithm, which adapts the learning rate for each parameter) and RMSProp (Root Mean Square propagation, which scales the learning rate by a moving average of recent gradient magnitudes).Adam maintains two moving averages for each parameter:

  • First Moment (Mean): Keeps track of the mean of the gradients.
  • Second Moment (Uncentered Variance): Keeps track of the variance of the gradients.

These two moments are used to adapt the learning rate for each parameter, making Adam well-suited for dealing with sparse gradients, noisy data, and large datasets. Based on the learning rate, the weights of the model are updated with respect to the gradient. A small learning rate (like 0.001) ensures that the model converges slowly, making it less likely to overshoot the minimum of the loss function. We initialize the Adam optimizer with a learning rate of 0.001 and applies it to the parameters of the Q-network (q_net) as follows:optimizer = optim.Adam(q_net.parameters(), lr=0.001)Generally, the metric to track the quality of model training is the loss function, which is the difference between target value and model predicted value. In context of the DQN algorithm, and specifically here, the loss function is the mean squared error loss, which is the mean squared error (MSE) between the Q-values predicted by the Q-network and the target values computed by the Bellman equation. We initialize the MSEloss function as:loss_fn = nn.MSELoss()

Step 7 – Replay Memory

A ReplayMemory class is implemented to store and manage experiences (transitions) encountered by the agent. This memory allows the agent to sample and reuse past experiences during training, which helps break the correlation between consecutive experiences and stabilize learning.

class ReplayMemory:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.memory = deque(maxlen=capacity)
    def push(self, transition):
        self.memory.append(transition)
    def sample(self, batch_size):
        return random.sample(self.memory, batch_size)
    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.memory)

The memory is implemented as a deque (double-ended queue) with a fixed capacity. The push(transition) function adds a new experience to the memory. The sample(batch_size) function randomly samples a batch of experiences for training, and __len__ method returns the current size of the memory.To train DQN, there are a few operations that need to be repeated iteratively for every episode during the training loop. For explaining them, let us modularize them and show in steps 8 through 13 in the following sub-sections .

Step 8 – Environment reset

At the start of each episode, we reset the environment, initializing the state, total reward, and done flag. The states can be randomized or have some gradual changing values depending on the training strategy.

def initialize_environment(env):
    state, _ = env.reset()
    total_reward = 0
    done = False
    return state, total_reward, done

Step 9 – Select Action:

In previous chapters, we discussed importance of using an exploration-exploitation strategy. We will continue to use epsilon greedy strategy, where for a small probability, we will randomly pick an action within the allowed range, and for the rest of the times we will pick the action that has maximum Q value. The implementation of the select_action function is given below.

def select_action(q_net, state, epsilon, env):
    if random.random() > epsilon:
        with torch.no_grad():
            action = q_net(state).max(1)[1].item()
    else:
        action = env.action_space.sample()
    return action

Step 10 – Store Experience:

During training based on DQN algorithm, we store experiences to a replay buffer and sample them as a batch from there for updating Q-network. The store experience function stores data at each iteration consistently to the replay buffer.

def store_experience(memory, state, action, reward, next_state, done):
    memory.push((state, action, reward, next_state, done))

Step 11 – Update Q-Network:

This function samples a batch of experiences from replay memory and computes the loss between the current Q-values and the expected Q-values. current_q_values are the predicted Q-values for the actions taken. max_next_q_values are the maximum predicted Q-value for the next state from the target network, and the expected_q_values are calculated using the Bellman equation. The loss is backpropagated to update the Q-network’s weights.

def update_q_network(q_net, target_net, memory, optimizer, loss_fn, gamma, batch_size):
    if len(memory) < batch_size:
        return
    transitions = memory.sample(batch_size)
    batch_state, batch_action, batch_reward, batch_next_state, batch_done = zip(*transitions)
    batch_state_tensor = torch.FloatTensor(batch_state)
    batch_action_tensor = torch.LongTensor(batch_action).unsqueeze(1)
    batch_reward_tensor = torch.FloatTensor(batch_reward)
    batch_next_state_tensor = torch.FloatTensor(batch_next_state)
    batch_done_tensor = torch.FloatTensor(batch_done)
    current_q_values = q_net(batch_state_tensor).gather(1, batch_action_tensor)
    max_next_q_values = target_net(batch_next_state_tensor).max(1)[0].detach()
    expected_q_values = batch_reward_tensor + (gamma * max_next_q_values * (1 - batch_done_tensor))
    loss = loss_fn(current_q_values.squeeze(), expected_q_values)
    optimizer.zero_grad()
    loss.backward()
    optimizer.step()

If the replay memory has accumulated enough transitions (at least batch_size), a batch of experiences is sampled from the memory. The Q-network is then updated by:Computing the predicted Q-values for the current states and actions.Calculating the expected q values from predicted current q values from q_net and the predicted Q-values from the target_net for the next states.Computing the loss between the predicted and target Q-values using the specified loss function.Backpropagating the loss and updating the Q-network’s weights using the optimizer.

Step 12 – Update Target Network

To keep up with the progress made by the predictor, the target network needs to be periodically updated. We implement an update function for the target network also.

def update_target_network(q_net, target_net):
    target_net.load_state_dict(q_net.state_dict())

Note that by update, here we are simply updating the weights of the network.

Step 13 – Decay Epsilon

As the network gathers experience based on training episodes, and makes progress in its policies, we schedule a reduction in exploration and let the agent do more exploitation. The decay function decays the value of epsilon over time to reduce exploration as the agent becomes more confident in its actions.

def decay_epsilon(epsilon, epsilon_end, epsilon_start, episode, epsilon_decay):
    return max(epsilon_end, epsilon_start - episode / epsilon_decay)

Now that we got all modularized steps written as functions, we can use then in the training loop. The next section discusses the main training loop.

Training Loop

The main training loop in the DQN algorithm is where the actual learning happens. The loop iteratively interacts with the environment, collects experiences, and uses these experiences to update the Q-network. Here are the steps within the training loop, using the above functions and classes:

  1. Inputs and Configurations: We first assign or input all the training configuration parameters to the function.
def train_dqn(q_net, target_net, env, optimizer, loss_fn, num_episodes=1000, gamma=0.99,
              epsilon_start=1.0, epsilon_end=0.1, epsilon_decay=500, batch_size=32, target_update_freq=10):
  1. Initialize Replay Memory and Epsilon: A ReplayMemory instance is initialized to store experiences (state, action, reward, next_state, done) that the agent collects as it interacts with the environment. epsilon_start sets the initial value for epsilon, which controls the exploration-exploitation trade-off. Higher epsilon means more exploration (random actions), while lower epsilon leads to more exploitation (actions based on the Q-network’s knowledge).
    memory = ReplayMemory(capacity=10000)
    epsilon = epsilon_start
    rewards = []
  1. Loop Over Episodes: The loop runs for num_episodes, where each episode is an attempt by the agent to solve the task or environment (e.g., move the car to the top of the hill in the MountainCar-v0 environment).
    for episode in range(num_episodes):
  1. Initialize the environment and start episode loop: At the start of each episode, the environment is initialized by resetting to its initial state using env.reset(), and the agent's total reward for the episode is set to 0. The done flag is initialized to False to indicate that the episode is ongoing. The episode runs in a while loop until conditions are met, that is done = True. The state output from gym environment initialization is converted to a tensor type.
        state, total_reward, done = initialize_environment(env)
        while not done:
            state_tensor = torch.FloatTensor(state).unsqueeze(0)
  1. Action selection and assignment: The agent selects an action based on the current state. This decision is made using the epsilon-greedy strategy: With probability epsilon, the agent explores by choosing a random action. For rest of the cases with probability 1-epsilon, the agent exploits what it learned by choosing the action that maximizes the Q-value predicted by the q_net for the current state.
            action = select_action(q_net, state_tensor, epsilon, env)
  1. Execute action and observe states: The selected action is executed in the environment using env.step(action), which returns:
    • next_state: The state the environment transitions to.
    • reward: The reward received for taking the action.
    • done: A boolean flag indicating if the episode has ended.
    • trunc: A flag for episode truncation (can be treated as done).
    • info: Additional information provided by the environment (often ignored).
            next_state, reward, done, trunc, info = env.step(action)
            done = done or trunc
  1. Store Transition in Replay Memory: The transition (state, action, reward, next_state, done) is stored in the replay memory. This experience will be used later for training the Q-network.
            store_experience(memory, state, action, reward, next_state, done)
  1. Optimize and update: The state is first updated with next state, similarly total reward is updated, and then the q network is optimized and updated.
            state = next_state
            total_reward += reward
            update_q_network(q_net, target_net, memory, optimizer, loss_fn, gamma, batch_size)
  1. Update the Target Network: Every periodic increase of the number of episodes by target update frequency, target_update_freq, the weights of the q_net are copied to the target_net. This step ensures stability in learning by decoupling the network used for estimating future rewards from the network being updated.
        if episode % target_update_freq == 0:
            update_target_network(q_net, target_net)
  1. Decay Epsilon: Epsilon is decayed after each episode to gradually shift the agent's behavior from exploration to exploitation. This decay is crucial for allowing the agent to learn from its experiences and rely more on the Q-network as it improves:
        epsilon = decay_epsilon(epsilon, epsilon_end, epsilon_start, episode, epsilon_decay)
  1. Log and Store Results: The total reward for each episode is stored in the rewards list. Additionally, progress is logged every 100 episodes, showing the episode number, total reward, and current epsilon value.
        rewards.append(total_reward)
        if episode % 100 == 0:
            print(f'Episode {episode}, Total Reward: {total_reward}, Epsilon: {epsilon:.2f}')

At this point the rewards are returned as output:

    return rewards

In addition to training function, a few more functions are necessary for re-using the training models. For complex environments, training can be a time-consuming process, and saving the model, plotting the training progress, and logging outputs might be necessary. Similarly, before deploying trained models, loading the trained model and running inference on trained models might be necessary. We will cover those additional functions next.

Additional Model Training Utilities

To track the training progress, not lose training progress, and analyze the trained agents model learnings, we now discuss some model training utilities. These are custom functionalities, and you can create them in several ways. Here we will discuss one specific way of saving the model, loading the model, plotting some variables such as rewards and logging variables so they can be plotted and inspected as required.

  1. Saving the Model

The save_model function is used to save the trained model's parameters (weights and biases) to a file. This allows you to save the state of the model after training, so you can load it later without retraining from scratch. model.state_dict() method returns a dictionary containing the model's parameters. The state dictionary includes all the learnable parameters (e.g., weights and biases) of the model's layers.

def save_model(model, path):
    torch.save(model.state_dict(), path)

The torch.save function takes the state dictionary and saves it to a file specified by path. This file is typically saved with a .pth or .pt extension, which is a common convention in PyTorch.

  1. Load Model

The load_model function is used to load the model's parameters from a file, restoring the model to its previously saved state for further use, such as inference or additional training. torch.load(path) loads the state dictionary (i.e., the model's parameters) from the file specified by path.

def load_model(model, path):
    model.load_state_dict(torch.load(path))
    model.eval()

model.load_state_dict(...) method loads the state dictionary into the model, setting the model's parameters to the values that were saved. model.eval()method sets the model to evaluation mode. In evaluation mode, certain layers like dropout and batch normalization behave differently compared to training mode, ensuring the model operates correctly when making predictions.

  1. Plot Rewards

The plot_rewards function is used to visualize the total rewards accumulated by the agent over the episodes during training. This plot helps to assess the agent's learning progress.

def plot_rewards(rewards):
    plt.plot(range(len(rewards)), rewards)
    plt.xlabel('Episode')
    plt.ylabel('Total Reward')
    plt.title('Training Rewards over Episodes')
    plt.show()
  1. Logging

To keep track of model quality and progress, some type of analytics and visualization beyond plotting reward progress can be useful. For this, we add a logging function.

import logging
def log_training_info(episode, action, reward, state, next_state, done, trunc):
    logging.info(f"Episode: {episode}, Action: {action}, Reward: {reward}, "
                 f"State: {state}, Next State: {next_state}, Done: {done}, Trunc: {trunc}")

This logging function can be added within the training loop right after the env.step() method.These utilities enhance the overall workflow by providing tools to manage and analyze the model effectively. Training the DQN script python torch_dqn.py --train, using the execution code as below, will train the agent, and save the q network model:

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(args.train)
    if args.train:
        rewards = train_dqn(q_net, target_net, env, optimizer, loss_fn)
        # # Saving the model
        save_model(q_net, 'q_network_tgt_test.pth')
        # # Plotting training rewards
        plot_rewards(rewards)
    else:
        load_model(q_net, 'q_network_tgt_test.pth')
        inference(env,q_net, episodes=10)

The plot of total reward accumulated per episode vs number of episodes during training should look similar to below:

The plot shows that total reward does not progress until around 300 episodes. This indicates that the reward assigned by OpenAI gym for car climbing the mountain and reaching the flag is sparse, and only happens at the end of the episode. During iterations, the agent does not receive high reward until it climbs and reaches the goal. The replay buffer collects experiences, and these experiences help learn from good experiences with high rewards. We could shape the reward to improve training, but this is not always possible for every problem, without understanding the problem in depth. This is where human subject matter expertise plays a role. Since we are focusing on algorithmic aspects of RL in this chapter, and human reward models in general for this book, we will rely on OpenAI gym rewards.The example of mountain car with continuous states and discrete actions shows how deep neural networks can enable scaling of value-based learning to more complex or large state space problems. The DQN algorithm does not directly train the policy, but does it indirectly by first training the Q network. The algorithms considered here train the value function or action value function (the Q network) first using data stored in a replay buffer and then update the target network separately instead of using data from current policy directly, and therefore represent the class of off-policy algorithms.Notice that the actions output is still discrete, and DQN while allowing large or continuous state spaces, does not support continuous actions. There are some modified algorithms that train value functions and can be used for outputting continuous actions. Some of these are deep deterministic policy gradients (DDPG) and more scalable distributed distributional deep deterministic policy gradient(D4PG).If you are interested in diving deeper, please refer https://spinningup.openai.com/en/latest/user/algorithms.xhtml for understanding core algorithms from reliability and sample efficiency perspectives, and for further details on distribution and scaling refer to https://arxiv.org/pdf/2006.00979. While these algorithms are remarkably sample efficient (requiring lesser number of samples to train) and solve the problem, they can suffer from three outstanding issues:

  • stability issues when updating from Q-network to target network,
  • value function overestimation of the expected return due to the max operator in the update rule (in case of Q function),
  • distribution mismatch between the policy used to collect data and the policy being updated.

Our general focus here is on RLHF and reward modeling based approaches, and there is no guarantee on stationarity of rewards as discussed in the paper Deep Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1706.03741. For such cases on non-stationary rewards, instability of algorithms can be an issue. The problem of instability coming from reward model for off-policy algorithms is addressed to some extent by relabeling all the agent’s past experiences every update of the reward model, as presented in these papers such as PEBBLE https://arxiv.org/pdf/2106.05091, and https://arxiv.org/pdf/1811.06521.However, considerations of scalability to complex environments, overhead of unsupervised pretraining, and quality of relabeling cannot be ignored. On-policy algorithms are relatively more stable, have shown more promise in past works such as Generative Adversarial Imitation Learning (GAIL): https://arxiv.org/pdf/1606.03476 and have empirically proven to be effective in practice, for example ChatGPT and several other products made their PPO implementation a state of the art in RLHF.In the next section, we will introduce some on-policy algorithms that accommodate using continuous actions. In you are interested in more RL implementations, check out the repositories such as OpenAI Baselines https://github.com/openai/baselines/tree/master and Stable Baselines3 https://stable-baselines3.readthedocs.io/en/master/ for high quality implementations, structured with breadth in mind, and CleanRL https://docs.cleanrl.dev/ for single file implementations structured to help readers to go through implementation details.

Policy Training Methods: Policy Gradients and Actor-Critic Methods

In this section we will focus on policy gradient algorithms, which is a different class of algorithms. These algorithms require the agent to explore the environment and update its policy based on the actions taken and the rewards received during this exploration, rather than from past experiences as in off-policy methods like Deep Q-Learning (DQN). This section introduces two prominent on-policy algorithms: Policy Gradients and Advantage Actor-Critic (A2C), and form the conceptual building blocks on top of which the more advanced algorithms such as proximal policy optimization (PPO) were developed. We will cover the main concepts here and apply them to PPO.

Policy Gradient Methods

In policy gradient methods, the algorithm directly optimizes the policy based on estimation of the gradient of the expected reward with respect to parameters defining the policy. That means instead of generating policy from the value function as is the case of Q-learning, the policy is parametrized. If π represents the policy, as in previous chapters, we represent it as , where Ѳ parametrizes the policy. The main idea is to adjust the policy parameters in the direction that maximizes the expected cumulative reward. The policy is often parameterized using a neural network. The policy represents the probability of taking action a given state s with parameters θ. The objective function is , where R is cumulative reward obtained by following the policy , and objective is to maximize expected reward. The objective function can be written more explicitly for understanding better as the sum over all possible states and actions, weighted by their probabilities and the reward obtained.Here P(s,a) is the joint probability of being in state s and taking action a, and R is the total reward accumulated over an entire trajectory or episode. To maximize , we need to compute its gradient with respect to the policy parameters Ѳ. The likelihood ratio method helps in deriving this gradient.Gradient of the objective function with respect to Ѳ can be represented as:Notice that the gradient of such an objective function may not be easily tractable, and we have to use some mathematical tricks such as Log likelihood.

Log Likelihood Method

From Calculus, we know that . The logarithm of the probability function transforms the product of probabilities into a sum, which is easier to handle. So, we replace gradient as . Another reason this helps is when gradients of small probabilities lead to numerical instability, logarithms prevent underflow, and only lead to negative numbers. It also makes computation of derivatives easier.

Policy Update Rule

The computed gradients of policy are used to update the policy parameters using gradient ascent.The updated policy function can be updates of neural network weights, and output of continuous actions distribution. This is suitable for continuous action problems.<>

Advantage Actor Critic: Variance Reduction using Baseline Subtraction

Vanilla policy gradients use the total return (sum of discounted rewards) to estimate the gradient of the policy. This return can be highly variable due to the stochastic nature of the environment and the agent's actions.
