High variance in the gradient estimates can lead to unstable training, where the policy updates can fluctuate wildly. This instability can make it difficult for the algorithm to converge to an optimal policy. Learning can be slow because the updates are noisy and less reliable.To address this problem, instead of using the total discounted reward for each action, subtract a baseline to reduce variance. The baseline is typically an estimate of the expected return (e.g., the value function), and the difference between the reward and baseline is known as the advantage. We want to encourage actions that are better than average. Using the advantage function, reduces variance by reducing the magnitude of policy updates for actions that have average or expected rewards. A simple way of doing baseline subtraction is to define advantage function as:To estimate value function, we add a network that predicts values, which can be used to compute advantage. The value function is average expected return, and by using it as a baseline subtracted from total returns, makes sure that actions that lead to returns above average are considered. This new network is termed as the Critic network, and the policy network is termed the Actor network.Incorporating the baseline subtraction trick using the advantage function A(s,a)=R−V(s) into the vanilla policy gradient algorithm reduces variance in the policy gradient estimates, leading to more stable and efficient learning. The addition of the value network (critic) allows the algorithm to better approximate the expected returns from each state, which results in more accurate policy updates.Using Q(s,a) instead of R, gives us a more precise capture of the advantage, as it directly reflects how much better or worse an action is compared to the average for that state under the policy. So, the more accurate advantage function would be:In this latter case, both Q and V are learned to compute advantages.

Generalized Advantage Estimation

Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE) is a technique used to improve the stability and efficiency of reinforcement learning algorithms. It's particularly useful in policy gradient methods and actor-critic algorithms. The main goal of GAE is to reduce the variance of the policy gradient estimates while keeping the bias relatively low. It uses two fundamental concepts of reinforcement learning:

  1. The advantage function A(s,a), determines how much better or worse taking action a in state s is compared to the average action taken when in that state. The accurate formulation is .
  2. Temporal Difference (TD) Error: The TD error updates the value function in actor-critic methods. It's the difference between the predicted value and the actual return: .

GAE introduces a more general way to compute the advantage function using a combination of TD errors with a parameter λ to control the trade-off between bias and variance. The GAE advantage estimate is given by:GAE involves modification to advantage actor critic (A2C) algorithm, and is an upgraded version. Instead of computing discounted rewards, values, and resulting advantages, a function computing the GAE estimate is used.These concepts are building blocks for on-policy RL algorithms. Note that, the chronology of development from VPG to A2C to GAE, has been a continuous improvement, and these early algorithms and concepts, are not enough to solve more challenging problems, as they still do not resolve stability issues completely. Moreover, they require a large amount of training and experimentation of hyperparameters.More sophisticated ideas have evolved over the last few years in addition to generalized advantage estimators introduced in the papers High-Dimensional Continuous Control Using Generalized Advantage Estimation: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1506.02438, e.g.Trust Region Policy Optimization (TRPO): https://arxiv.org/abs/1502.05477 and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) Algorithms: https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.06347 that help improve stability and sensitivity to hyperparameter tuning, and latter (TRPO and PPO) guarantee monotonic convergence also. By discussing DQN, VPG, A2C, GAE, we have covered most of the critical fundamental building blocks of variant algorithms in RL, and are ready for understand the more advanced PPO algorithm. We will discuss application of PPO, as it plays an important role in RLHF based model tuning state of the art methodologies.

Application of Proximal Policy Optimization

PPO is an important algorithm from the perspective of RLHF. In this section, we discuss the key upgrades used to develop PPO on top of previously discussed, vanilla policy gradients, advantage actor-critic, and generalized advantage estimates, followed by application of PPO to the continuous mountain car problem. Since all the algorithms we discussed previously are subsets of PPO, we will directly consider its implementation. We will first introduce the continuous mountain car environment, followed by implementing PPO.

Introducing Continuous Mountain Car Simulation Environment

In the discrete action Mountain Car environment, the agent has a limited set of predefined actions (e.g., move left, move right, do nothing). However, in the continuous version, the agent can choose from a wide range of actions, which is more realistic for many real-world problems. Here, we will use the continuous version of the mountain car simulator. The difference here is that actions can be any number between -1 and 1. There are more details on simulator details documented in https://gymnasium.farama.org/environments/classic_control/mountain_car_continuous/, but we will discuss the relevant system dynamics and rewards for completeness.In case of discrete action Mountain Car, there is a negative reward of -1 for every timestep, until the episode terminates when the car climbs the mountain and reaches the flag. It is a sparse scenario but incentivized to keep episodes short. For the continuous-action mountain car, a negative reward of -0.1 x action2 is received at each timestep to penalize for taking actions of large magnitude. If the mountain car reaches the goal, then a positive reward of +100 is added to the negative reward for that timestep. Since large actions also create momentum and help with climbing and reaching the goal, this is a more difficult credit assignment. In real-world scenarios, assigning rewards can be difficult so, we will continue to use rewards from the gymnasium library for keeping focus on the algorithm here in lieu reward shaping.In previous sections we realized that DQN is effective for discrete action spaces, but cannot be directly applied to continuous action spaces because DQN would require discretizing the action space, which isn't ideal for environments with a large or infinite number of possible actions.Policy gradient methods on the contrary are a natural solution for continuous action spaces, since they allow the agent to learn a parameterized policy that can produce actions from a continuous range by modeling the action probability distribution. The vanilla policy gradient algorithm uses a stochastic policy to map states to actions, optimizing this policy using rewards from the environment. However, it requires baseline subtraction to reduce variance, as discussed in advantage actor critic algorithm, and further improved stabilization of learning using generalized advantage estimates (GAE). To further improve learning stability and monotonic improvement, we add further improvements on top of GAE, using PPO algorithm, one of the key updates being a clipping mechanism to prevent large updates in policy.Continuous control mountain car can have a wider exploration region of states and actions, leading a more complex learning and requiring longer simulation and training. Therefore, we need to apply some techniques for accelerating training. One of the common approaches is using vectorized environments that parallelizes training, as we will discuss in the following section.

Accelerating Training Using Vectorized Environments

To accelerate training, it is very common in RL practices to use vectorized environments. Vectorization involves setup where multiple independent instances of the same environment are run simultaneously. Instead of interacting with a single environment at a time, the agent interacts with several environments in parallel. Each environment operates independently and might be in a different state. Since these environments run in parallel, either on separate CPU cores or using efficient multiprocessing techniques, the time spent waiting for the environment's feedback (i.e., the next observation and reward) is reduced significantly. In addition, the agent can collect a larger batch of experiences in a shorter period. The Gymnasium library provides built-in support for vectorized environments through the gym.vector module.A simple code example of implementing vectorized environments for mountain car continuous action problem with random action samples is as below snippet of code. This below code first imports the gymnasium library, and creates a vectorized environment with 4 copies of MountainCar environment, specifically for t’MountainCarContinuous-v0' version. The observations for all of the environments are collected by resetting the vectorized environments. For demonstration purposes, we run 100 iterations or number of steps of the vectorized environments, and for each iteration first sample the actions randomly using the action_space.sample method for each of the environments, and step through each of the them using the step method. Then, we handle each of the environment episodes based on whether dones variables is True, and print the terminated episodes and corresponding rewards. In the complete implementation, you can log these and also plot them to monitor episodes and debug training if required. This is followed by closing the environment using close method.

import gymnasium as gym
env = gym.vector.make(‘MountainCarContinuous-v0', num_envs=4)
observations = env.reset()
for _ in range(100):
    actions = env.action_space.sample()
    observations, rewards, dones, infos = env.step(actions)
    for i in range(4):
        if dones[i]:
            print(f"Episode {i} finished with reward {rewards[i]}")
# Close the environments
env.close()

We will use the vectorized environments for training the agent with PPO algorithm, as you will see in PPO Implementation Details section. First we will look at all the key updates required for implementing PPO in practice.

Key Updates for Implementing PPO

Training of continuous action spaces is relatively more difficult in comparison to discrete action spaces, as it has a much larger action space. As a result, the training time for continuous action environments is much longer than for discrete action environments. To accelerate training, and improve training stability and other training related improvements, several techniques are implemented for PPO algorithms. We will go over these:

  • Clipped Objective Function (Surrogate Loss): Introduces a clipped objective to prevent large updates to the policy, which can lead to instability. The ratio of new policy probability to old policy probability is clipped within a certain range, ensuring that updates are small and controlled.
  • Multiple Epochs per Batch: Allows multiple epochs of optimization on the same batch of data, which increases the sample efficiency. The policy is updated several times using the same data, extracting more information from each collected batch.
  • ,Value Function Loss: Adds more flexibility by allowing separate learning rates for the policy (actor) and the value function (critic). This allows fine-tuning of learning for each component, leading to better convergence. (PPO also optionally includes a clipped value function loss, similar to the policy objective clipping, to avoid overfitting the value function to the current policy.)
  • Entropy Regularization: Maintains exploration by encouraging a certain level of randomness in the policy, preventing it from becoming too deterministic.
  • Mini-batch Updates: Uses mini-batch updates, which further increases sample efficiency and stabilizes learning. Smaller mini-batches allow more gradient updates and smoother policy changes over time.

PPO Implementation Details

Since PPO algorithm has considerable number of implementation details, we will structure it as a project with following file structure:

/ppo /
    ├── agent.py           # Contains the Agent class
    ├── training.py        # Contains training logic
    ├── utils.py           # Contains utility functions (save/load models)
    ├── env_setup.py       # Contains environment setup logic
    ├── args.yaml        # Contains training parameters
    ├── main.py            # Main script to run the training
├── inference.py            # Main script to run the training

All the required files are available in the GitHub repository linked at the beginning of the chapter. But, we will go over important aspects of the project files here. We have adopted the project implementation from cleanRL, as it is the most transparent implementation and clearer for understanding, learning or experimenting at finest level. Understanding this code structure will help you understand all concepts of PPO and allow you to make changes and experiment in a transparent way. We will now discuss all important implementation aspects from the code structure.

Agent Class (agent.py)

The Agent class combines two main components: an actor and a critic. The actor suggests actions to take based on the current state of the environment, while the critic evaluates how good those actions are, providing feedback to improve future decision-making. We will go over all the methods from Agent class as follows.

Initialization (__init__ method)

The constructor takes a vectorized environment (envs) as input. This allows the agent to operate in either a single environment or multiple environments simultaneously. This method checks whether the environment is a vectorized one and extracts the shape of the observation and action spaces.The agent determines if the action space is discrete (e.g., selecting from a finite set of actions) or continuous (e.g., producing a range of values). This distinction affects how actions are sampled and evaluated. The following snippet shows the initialization method.

def __init__(self, envs):
    super().__init__()
        if isinstance(envs, vector.VectorEnv):
            # Multi-environment case
            env_shape = envs.single_observation_space.shape
            env_action_shape = envs.single_action_space.shape
        else:
           # Single environment case
            env_shape = envs.observation_space.shape
            env_action_shape = envs.action_space.shape
        # Determine if the action space is discrete or continuous
        if len(env_action_shape) == 1 and env_action_shape[0] > 1:
            # Discrete action space
            self.is_discrete = True
            self.num_actions = env_action_shape[0]
        elif len(env_action_shape) == 1 and env_action_shape[0] == 1:
            # Single action case; could be treated as continuous
            self.is_discrete = False
        else:
            # Assume continuous action space
            self.is_discrete = False
Critic Network

The critic is defined as a neural network that takes the state as input and outputs a single value, representing the estimated value of that state. This network has two hidden layers, each with 64 units and Tanh activation functions. The final layer outputs a single value, initialized with a standard deviation of 1.0 to control the variance of the output. A larger standard deviation allows for more variability in the value outputs, which is essential for:

  • Flexibility in Value Estimation: It provides the critic with the capacity to learn a wider range of value estimates. This is particularly important in environments where the range of possible state values can be quite large, e.g. continuous environments such as the continuous mountain car, or any of the continuous control environment in the gymnasium library.
  • Preventing Early Convergence: By allowing a higher variance initially, the critic can adapt better to the learning dynamics of the agent, which helps in distinguishing between good and bad actions more effectively during training.
        # Define the critic network
        self.critic = nn.Sequential(
            self.layer_init(nn.Linear(np.prod(env_shape), 64)),
            nn.Tanh(),
            self.layer_init(nn.Linear(64, 64)),
            nn.Tanh(),
            self.layer_init(nn.Linear(64, 1), std=1.0),
        )
Actor Network

Similar structure to the critic but outputs the mean action. The last layer is initialized with a small standard deviation, a small value means that the actions sampled from the actor's output will be very close to the mean action. In practical terms, the agent will produce actions that are tightly clustered around this mean, leading to consistent and less variable actions.

        # Define the actor network
        self.actor_mean = nn.Sequential(
            self.layer_init(nn.Linear(np.prod(env_shape), 64)),
            nn.Tanh(),
            self.layer_init(nn.Linear(64, 64)),
            nn.Tanh(),
            self.layer_init(nn.Linear(64, np.prod(env_action_shape)), std=0.01),
        )
        # For continuous action space
        if not self.is_discrete:
            self.actor_logstd = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(1, np.prod(env_action_shape)))

In practice, the combination of these two standard deviations of actor and critic supports a training process where the agent starts by exploring a wider range of actions and then gradually stabilizes around effective actions while still receiving diverse feedback on the value of different states.

Handling Continuous Actions

For continuous actions, an additional parameter for the action’s standard deviation is created. This allows the agent to model the uncertainty in continuous actions, which is important for effective sampling from the action distribution.

        # For continuous action space
        if not self.is_discrete:
            self.actor_logstd = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(1, np.prod(env_action_shape)))

Instead of directly using a standard deviation, the actor maintains a log standard deviation parameter, which allows for numerical stability (logarithmic values are less likely to result in numerical underflow when calculating probabilities) and flexibility (it can be adjusted during training, allowing the agent to learn to explore more if necessary).

Methods

The get_value method takes a state as input and returns its estimated value from the critic network.

    def get_value(self, x):
        """Returns the value of the input state."""
        return self.critic(x)

The get_action_and_value method computes the action to take based on the current state and returns the selected action, which could be sampled (for exploration) or provided (for evaluation) using log probability of that action This sampling can be useful for calculating the loss during training, the entropy of the action distribution, promoting exploration by encouraging the agent to take a diverse set of actions, and the value of the input state from the critic, providing feedback on how favorable that state is for the agent.

    def get_action_and_value(self, x, action=None):
        action_mean = self.actor_mean(x)
        if self.is_discrete:
            # For discrete action spaces, use a softmax layer
            action_probs = nn.functional.softmax(action_mean, dim=-1)
            if action is None:
                action = action_probs.multinomial(num_samples=1).squeeze(1)
            log_prob = action_probs.log()[range(action.size(0)), action]
            entropy = -(action_probs * action_probs.log()).sum(dim=-1)
        else:
            # For continuous action spaces
            action_logstd = self.actor_logstd.expand_as(action_mean)
            action_std = torch.exp(action_logstd)
            probs = Normal(action_mean, action_std)
            if action is None:
                action = probs.sample()
            log_prob = probs.log_prob(action).sum(1)
            entropy = probs.entropy().sum(1)
        return action, log_prob, entropy, self.critic(x)

For discrete actions, the Softmax Layer computes probabilities for each action, the action sampling samples an action or uses the provided action, and then calculates log probability and entropy for training. For continuous actions, the normal distribution uses the mean and standard deviation to define a Gaussian distribution for actions, action sampling samples from this distribution if no action is provided, and then calculates log probability and entropy.The Layer Initialization (layer_init method) method initializes the neural network layers with specific configurations. It uses orthogonal initialization for weights to stabilize training and sets a constant value for biases, ensuring that the layers are prepared for effective learning.

@staticmethod
def layer_init(layer, std=np.sqrt(2), bias_const=0.0):
    nn.init.orthogonal_(layer.weight, std)
    nn.init.constant_(layer.bias, bias_const)
    return layer

The Agent class effectively combines actor-critic methods, adapting to both discrete and continuous action spaces. Each component—from initialization to action selection—plays a crucial role in how the agent learns and interacts with the environment. This structured approach allows the agent to efficiently learn policies that maximize rewards through reinforcement learning. Note that here we are adopting the cleanRL architecture, rather than experimenting with a new one. But, for a problem such as continuous mountain car with sparse rewards, a model architecture involving memory such as LSTM (long short term memory) or GRU (gated recurrent units) can help retain knowledge from earlier states of the episode. For now, we will continue with the standard architecture used in the libraries.

Training Parameters (args.yaml)

PPO algorithm has several training parameters that range from env_id and num_envs related to environment, to number of steps for training num_steps, to learning rate learning_rate. All these are added in a separate configuration file called args.yaml. This helps organize the code and these can be used for experimenting with. Here, we are using the parameters adopted from cleanRL repository, and are tuned already.

PPO Training (training.py):

The detailed training loop is provided in the training.py file in the github repository. Here we explain key algorithmic steps of the training loop for PPO.Training Setup and InitializationPPO training requires preparing the parameters for training progress. Considering that we are using vectorized environments, this involves defining the following parameters:

  • Batch Size, total number of time steps collected across all environments.
  • Minibatch Size, for each update of the network, this is the size of the minibatch sampled from the collected batch,
  • Iterations, total number of iterations depends on how many time steps we plan to take across training.
batch_size = int(args['num_envs'] * args['num_steps'])
minibatch_size = batch_size // args['num_minibatches']
num_iterations = args['total_timesteps'] // batch_size

The goal is to prepare the optimizer and define how training progresses.

Checkpointing and Restarting

For long training sessions, it is common that training might need to be stopped, evaluated using inference, and continued further or resumed from where it was stopped. This helps with not having to restart training from scratch.

latest_checkpoint = get_latest_checkpoint(checkpoint_dir)
if latest_checkpoint:
    checkpoint = load_model(agent, latest_checkpoint)
    global_step = checkpoint.get('global_step', 0)
    start_iteration = checkpoint.get('start_iteration', 0)
else:
    global_step = 0
    start_iteration = 0

Checkpoints of the model are saved at regular intervals so that training progress is saved and further training can continue from the last saved model (checkpoint).

Annealing Learning Rate

Since our goal is to have stable learning, it is often beneficial to reduce the learning at later stages of training.

if args['anneal_lr']:
    frac = 1.0 - (iteration - 1.0) / num_iterations
    optimizer.param_groups[0]["lr"] = frac * args['learning_rate']

The learning rate is gradually decreased over time, encouraging stable convergence. This is typical in PPO to avoid excessive exploration in later stages of training.

Collecting Rollouts

Rollouts involve letting the environment step through a number of steps, generating data (obs, actions, logprobs, rewards, values), which can be collected for training.

for step in range(args['num_steps']):
    with torch.no_grad():
        action, logprob, _, value = agent.get_action_and_value(next_obs)
    next_obs, reward, terminations, truncations, infos = envs.step(action.cpu().numpy())
    rewards[step] = torch.tensor(reward).to(device).view(-1)

Note that torch.no_grad() ensures that we are not calculating gradients here, as we are only collecting data. Another point to note is that some environments might terminate sooner than others. For example, some successful episodes might climb the mountain and reach the flag and terminate. Since we are using vectorized environments, the training of environments does not end. But we are keeping a log of terminations and truncations, and using them in calculating advantages and returns.

Compute Advantages and Returns

As discussed in previous section, for stability of learning, we need to calculate advantages and returns. Here, GAE helps compute a more stable advantage by reducing variance in the reward signal.

next_value = agent.get_value(next_obs).reshape(1, -1)
advantages, returns = compute_advantages_and_returns(rewards, values, next_value, next_done, dones, args)

Here is how GAE is implemented to compute advantages and returns. After making appropriate device selection and advantages, a variable is initialized as a tensor filled with zeros that has the same shape as the rewards tensor. This tensor will store the computed advantage values for each timestep. This is followed by initialization of the last GAE lambda. This variable is used to keep track of the advantage calculated at the next timestep (moving backward through the trajectory). It starts at 0 for the last step. device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() and args['cuda'] else "cpu") advantages = torch.zeros_like(rewards).to(device) lastgaelam = 0Then a loop iterates backward over the time steps, from args['num_steps'] - 1 (the last step) down to 0. This reverse traversal is necessary for GAE, which propagates information from future rewards backward. In this look we are passing all of the environment variables and computing advantages and returns. As we discussed in the previous point, in vectorized environments, some episodes might have terminated earlier, and it is important to handle them properly. The key to handling terminated episodes lies in the use of the dones tensor, which indicates whether an episode has ended at a particular time step. In terminated episodes, the environment provides a done signal. This affects how future rewards are propagated, particularly by ensuring that after termination, the value of future rewards is cut off, which prevents future rewards from being propagated across episode boundaries.We create a new variable names nextnonterminal, which takes the value 1.0 when the episode has not terminated and 0.0 when the episode has terminated. If dones[t+1] (or next_done for the last step) is True (meaning the episode has terminated), nextnonterminal becomes 0.0, cutting off any future rewards and preventing further value propagation. This mechanism ensures that once an episode terminates, future rewards and values are not incorrectly added to the current step's advantages. Hence, we check for condition whether the step is last step of the episode, and assign nextnonterminal accordingly.

    for t in reversed(range(args['num_steps'])):
        if t == args['num_steps'] - 1:
            nextnonterminal = 1.0 - next_done
            nextvalues = next_value
        else:
            nextnonterminal = 1.0 - dones[t + 1]
            nextvalues = values[t + 1]

nextvalues in the above code snippet is set to next_value, when t is same as the last step of the episode or trajectory. The next_value is the value estimate for the next state, which is input to the function compute_advantages_and_returns. For all other steps it is set to the value estimate at the next timestep (t+1).In the loop, we then calculate the TD (Temporal Difference) residual, delta, using:

  • rewards[t]: the immediate reward at the current step,
  • args['gamma'] * nextvalues * nextnonterminal: the discounted value of the next step, considering whether the episode continues (nextnonterminal),
  • values[t]: the value estimate at the current step.

The difference between the sum of the immediate reward and the discounted future value and the current value estimate gives the TD error (delta).

delta = rewards[t] + args['gamma'] * nextvalues * nextnonterminal - values[t]

When nextnonterminal is 0.0 due to termination, the term args['gamma'] * nextvalues * nextnonterminal becomes 0.0. This means that the reward at time step t only considers the immediate reward rewards[t] and not the future value nextvalues from the next timestep.The advantage at step t is updated using GAE, which incorporates both the TD error (delta) and the advantage from the next timestep (lastgaelam), with discounting by gamma and weighting by gae_lambda. At the same time, lastgaelam is recursively updated with this new advantage value, to be used in the next iteration of the loop (moving backward). advantages[t] = lastgaelam = delta + args['gamma'] * args['gae_lambda'] * nextnonterminal * lastgaelam returns = advantages + values return advantages, returnsWhen an episode terminates, nextnonterminal is 0.0, meaning that lastgaelam (which carries over advantages from future timesteps) no longer contributes. The advantage at time step t depends only on the immediate TD residual (delta) rather than any future advantages. This ensures that GAE does not propagate advantages across episode boundaries, thus maintaining the integrity of the trajectory.The returns are then calculated by adding the estimated values to the computed advantages. This gives the total expected return (reward + future value) for each timestep. The function returns both advantages and returns, which can be used in the loss calculations.

Minibatch Sampling and PPO Update

In PPO, training is often repeated for several epochs. To achieve this, the collected batch is shuffled and split into minibatches for each epoch.

for epoch in range(args['update_epochs']):
    np.random.shuffle(b_inds)
    for start in range(0, batch_size, minibatch_size):
        mb_inds = b_inds[start:end]
        _, newlogprob, entropy, newvalue = agent.get_action_and_value(b_obs[mb_inds], b_actions[mb_inds])
        ratio = (newlogprob - b_logprobs[mb_inds]).exp()
        pg_loss1 = -mb_advantages * ratio

Policy Gradient Loss is the core PPO update and plays a central role in updating the agent's policy. It's formulated in a way that modifies the policy to increase the probability of taking actions that yield higher rewards, based on collected experiences. It uses a clipped objective to prevent large policy updates and maintain stability.Ratio measures the change between the old policy (logprob) and the new policy (newlogprob) for a specific action, where old logprob is the log-probability of the action under the policy before the update (from the previous iteration), and new logprob (newlogprob) is log-probability of the action under the current policy (after the update). The ratio allows us to measure how different the new policy is from the old one for the same action, and by using this ratio, PPO controls how much the policy can change at each step.PPO introduces clipping to prevent excessively large updates to the policy, which could destabilize training:

        pg_loss2 = -mb_advantages * torch.clamp(ratio, 1 - args['clip_coef'], 1 + args['clip_coef'])
        pg_loss = torch.max(pg_loss1, pg_loss2).mean()

Clipping ensures that the ratio stays within a reasonable range:, where is a small value, typically between 0.1 and 0.3. This prevents the policy from updating too aggressively (too far from the old policy).Two Loss Terms:pg_loss1 is the standard policy gradient, unmodified by any constraints, and pg_loss2 applies the clipping. If the ratio exceeds the bounds , the advantage is capped, preventing excessive updates.By taking the maximum of pg_loss1 and pg_loss2, PPO ensures that if the policy update is too large, it is restricted, thus maintaining stability.

Value Function and Entropy Loss

PPO is a policy-based method, but it also uses a value function to estimate the expected return from a given state. These two components work together, with the policy deciding actions and the value function predicting how good a state (or state-action pair) is.

v_loss = 0.5 * ((newvalue - b_returns[mb_inds]) ** 2).mean()
entropy_loss = entropy.mean()

Value Loss measures the difference between the predicted value (newvalue) and the actual return (computed from the advantage estimates) as Value Loss=. This is a standard mean squared error (MSE) between the predicted values and the observed returns. It helps the agent learn to better predict future rewards, improving the accuracy of its value estimates.Entropy Loss encourages exploration by preventing the policy from becoming too deterministic. When the agent’s policy assigns very high probabilities to a few actions and near-zero probabilities to others, it becomes overconfident and risks poor generalization. By adding an entropy bonus to the loss function, the policy is encouraged to keep its action probabilities more spread out, promoting exploration. The entropy loss can be denoted by the following equation:Entropy Loss = .The more uniform the action probabilities are, the higher the entropy. High entropy ensures the agent continues to explore various actions, especially in the early stages of training.The final loss combines the policy loss, value loss, and entropy loss:

loss = pg_loss - args['ent_coef'] * entropy_loss + v_loss * args['vf_coef']

pg_loss term is maximized to improve the agent’s policy based on the observed advantages, v_loss penalizes errors in the value function’s predictions, and entropy_loss adds a regularization term to ensure sufficient exploration. The coefficients args['vf_coef'] and args['ent_coef'] are hyperparameters that control the relative importance of the value loss and the entropy loss in the total loss function.

Gradient Clipping

The gradient clipping step is applied as it prevents exploding gradients by ensuring that the gradients do not grow too large, which can destabilize training. It is a standard deep learning training practice.

torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(agent.parameters(), args['max_grad_norm'])
KL Divergence Check

KL Divergence (Kullback-Leibler Divergence) is a measure of how one probability distribution diverges from another. This term essentially measures how much the updated policy has shifted from the original policy. A large KL divergence indicates a large change, while a small KL divergence means the policies are quite similar.

                if args['target_kl'] is not None:
                    with torch.no_grad():
                        approx_kl = ((ratio - 1) - logratio).mean()
                        clipfracs.append(((ratio - 1.0).abs() > args['clip_coef']).float().mean().item())
                    if approx_kl > args['target_kl']:
                        kl_exceed_count += 1
                        print(f"KL Divergence exceeded in iteration {iteration}, epoch {epoch}")
                        break  # Break out of the loop if KL exceeds

The PPO algorithm introduces clipping to limit the size of the policy update directly. However, clipping is an implicit control mechanism. KL Divergence monitoring provides an explicit control to detect when the policy update is too aggressive. This has two benefits:

  • Large Policy Shifts: If the new policy changes too drastically (i.e., the new action probabilities differ significantly from the old ones), it can lead to overfitting to short-term experiences and instability in training. Monitoring KL Divergence helps catch this.
  • Safe Updates: By monitoring KL divergence, PPO ensures the new policy is only slightly different from the old one, keeping updates within a "safe range." If the policy shift exceeds a predefined threshold, PPO can take corrective action, such as early stopping, reducing the learning rate, or halting updates temporarily.

While clipping focuses on restricting individual updates to the policy, KL divergence monitoring tracks the overall change in policy across the entire distribution. These two mechanisms complement each other:Clipping ensures that updates for individual actions do not deviate too much.KL Divergence Monitoring ensures that the overall policy doesn't shift too far in aggregate.

Logging and Saving

We use one of pytorch’s utility methods using tensorboard for logging important metrics like episode returns and episode lengths to the log for monitoring and visualization.

In addition, during training, we must periodically saves the model, ensuring progress is checkpointed and recoverable. Model saving can be computationally expensive, hence we condition the saving of model to occur during later half of the training.

if iteration % 2 == 0 or iteration == num_iterations:
    save_model(agent, f'checkpoints/ppo_checkpoint_{iteration}.pth', global_step, iteration)

At this point, we have covered all PPO training related functions, methods and implementation updates. Next we will go over execution code on how we implement the training runs using the main.py file.

Main.py

The overall execution of the training is run from main.py. Below is snippet of the code showing the steps

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Load config from YAML
    with open("args.yaml", "r") as f:
        args = yaml.safe_load(f)
    run_name = f"{args['env_id']}__{args['exp_name']}__{args['seed']}__{int(time.time())}"
    envs = gym.vector.SyncVectorEnv([make_env(args['env_id'], i, args['capture_video'], run_name, args['gamma']) for i in range(args['num_envs'])])
    # Initialize agent
    agent = Agent(envs)
    # Start training
    writer = SummaryWriter(f"runs/{run_name}")
    train(agent, envs, writer, args)
    envs.close()
    writer.close()

Running the PPO training, we see that it will converge to a high episode return of around 90. However, it is important to note that you may have to train for a long time, we are working with a sparse reward scenario, where high reward is only available at the end of the episode.The property of directly optimizing the policy based on gradients, makes PPO more effective in dealing with the sparse feedback than value-based methods like DQN. In addition, the clipped objective function helps maintain stability and reduces the risk of catastrophic policy updates, which can be beneficial when the feedback is inconsistent. Despite these benefits, the algorithm can become sample inefficient (meaning requires a large number of training samples or episodes) in sparse reward settings. If the agent doesn’t explore sufficiently or if exploration leads to poor initial policies, learning can stagnate. Since the algorithm optimizes based on sampled trajectories, if rewards are very sparse, it may require many more episodes before learning meaningful updates.

Sparse Rewards and Exploration

For PPO implementation above we used entropy regularization to help with exploration. The idea is to maximize the entropy of the policy distribution, which means encouraging the agent to maintain a diverse set of actions rather than converging to a deterministic policy too quickly. This approach promotes exploration dynamically throughout training, adapting based on the current policy distribution. Entropy regularization can stabilize learning by preventing premature convergence to suboptimal policies, allowing the agent to explore more diverse actions. The weight of the entropy term needs to be carefully tuned. Too much regularization can lead to excessive exploration, while too little may cause the agent to exploit suboptimal actions. Unlike epsilon-greedy, where there’s a guaranteed exploration rate, entropy regularization might lead to periods of suboptimal exploration if the policy converges too quickly.Comparison with epsilon greedy shows pros and cons of the two approaches can be seen in Table 4.1.

Epsilon-Greedy Entropy Regularization
Exploration mechanism Random action selection with ϵ Encourages diverse action distributions
Simplicity Simple to implement More complex due to tuning
Exploration Guarantees Fixed probability of exploration Adaptive exploration based on policy
Efficiency Can lead to inefficient exploration Can lead to more informed exploration
Tuning Epsilon decay schedule Weight of entropy term
Table 4. 1 Comparing epsilon-greedy and entropy regularization exploration strategies.

While the exploration strategies can help to some extent, sometimes reward sparsity is severe, and a more direct approach such as providing human guidance, or initializing with good or diverse set of policies can improve exploration. In the next section, we will discuss how human guidance can be incorporated in the form of behavioral cloning, along with PPO, and used for model initialization.

Using Human Guidance

In the last section, we explored various policy training techniques and implemented the PPO algorithm on the continuous action Mountain Car problem. The training takes a significantly large number of episode to achieve high returns. One of the main reasons is that the reward structure presents significant challenges: while the goal is to climb and reach the flag for a reward of +100, this conflicts with the necessity to limit action magnitudes (–0.1 * action²). The momentum created by larger actions aids in reaching the flag, yet we need to maintain smaller action magnitudes. Additionally, generating momentum requires consistent climbing, which demands extensive experience through many episodes.As humans, we inherently understand how we want a car to behave, yet defining an accurate reward function can be challenging. Much like human decision-making, which often requires evaluating the entire episode, an agent also benefits from comprehending the desired behavior in specific scenarios, such as climbing a mountain. For effective reward modeling, we typically gather preferences from multiple human evaluators to inform the reward function. However, individual actions and states at a single-step level are often insufficiently informative. Therefore, it may be necessary to adjust the training setup to consider sequences of actions at the episode level.An alternative approach is to initialize the agent's policy with a pretrained behavioral clone based on human expert trajectories. In this case, we will collect a set of human demonstrations and synthesize this data to enhance our training set. We will then employ behavioral cloning using the same agent architecture as in Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to replicate the demonstrated behavior. This process will initialize the agent for PPO training, enabling it to fine-tune its performance and generalize across scenarios not encountered during the initial demonstrations.We will discuss each step in policy initialization for PPO using behavioral cloning, in the Behavioral Cloning section. You can refer to the guided_ppo subdirectory in the github repository, which includes behavioral cloning related scripts in addition to previous files from PPO.

/guided_ppo /
    ├── agent.py           # Contains the Agent class
    ├── training.py        # Contains training logic
    ├── utils.py           # Contains utility functions (save/load models)
    ├── env_setup.py       # Contains environment setup logic
    ├── args.yaml        # Contains training parameters
    ├── main.py         # Main script to run the training
    ├── inference.py    # Main script to run the training
    ├── human_feedback_keyboard.py # enables human demonstrations
    ├── augment_expert_data.py # augment expert data by perturbing actions               
    ├── expert_behavior_cloning.py # train and inference behavioral clone of human expert
    ├── expert_trajectories.json # collected human trajectories
    ├── perturbed_expert_trajectories.json  # augmented data

Data Collection Process

We will utilize the Gym environment for the Mountain Car problem, employing the Pygame library to facilitate interaction via keyboard input. This allows human actions to directly influence the magnitude and direction of the car's movement. By collecting these human-demonstrated trajectories, we aim to enhance the reward structure for PPO training.The data collection process must be strategic; each action incurs a small negative reward, while only a sequence of actions leads to the eventual reward of +100. Random actions are inefficient for this task. Given that the equilibrium state of the Mountain Car environment lies at the valley bottom, many actions lead to the car descending rather than ascending. Instead of developing heuristics to help the car climb, we can redesign the environment to enable direct human interaction, allowing users to generate effective actions and policies.In this section, we will implement an interactive environment using Pygame and Gym to collect human demonstration data for training a reward model, focusing on essential code snippets to facilitate this process. The human_feedback_keyboard.py script to collect human demonstration is available in the github repository:

  1. After importing relevant libraries, we initialize the pygame, create the gym environment and reset it to the initial state:
pygame.init()
env = gym.make('MountainCarContinuous-v0', render_mode="human")
state, _ = env.reset()
  1. We write a docstring and print the instructions on how the user can interact with the game environment as follows:
instructions = """
Press 'a' to accelerate left,
'd' to accelerate right,
's' to stop,
'q' to quit.
"""
  1. Then, we set the main loop to keep the Pygame window responsive and check for a quit event.
running = True
while running:
    for event in pygame.event.get():
        if event.type == pygame.QUIT:
            running = False
  1. Then, we handle key presses using below event conditions:
keys = pygame.key.get_pressed()
if keys[pygame.K_a]:
    action = action_space.low[0]*np.random.uniform(0.2, 0.5)  # Accelerate left
elif keys[pygame.K_d]:
    action = action_space.high[0]*np.random.uniform(0.2, 0.5)  # Accelerate right
elif keys[pygame.K_s]:
    action = 0.0  # No acceleration (stop)
elif keys[pygame.K_q]:
    print("Quitting...")
    running = False  # Exit loop

Note that acceleration is set to action values randomized in a small low magnitude range so that less energy is used. This is in alliance with the reward goal of -0.1x action^2.

  1. With these human selected actions, we step through the environment loop, and continue until done=True. For each step, we record the state, action, next_state, reward, and done.
next_state, reward, done, _, _ = env.step([action])
episode.append({
    'state': state.tolist(),
    'action': action,
    'next_state': next_state.tolist(),
    'reward': reward,
    'done': done
})
  1. The running program checks for the done condition and saves the recorded trajectories to a JSON file for later analysis. Since we are just collecting a handful of trajectories, json format is sufficient. It also is human readable, so it is helpful to understand and use.
if done:
    state, _ = env.reset()
    trajectories.append(episode.copy())
else:
    state = next_state
with open('expert_trajectories.json', 'w') as f: json.dump(trajectories, f) print(f"Demonstrated trajectories saved to 'expert_trajectories.json'.")
  1. After collecting trajectories, the session ends by cleaning up the resources and closing the environment.
env.close()
pygame.quit()

Now that the data collection process is complete, we can discuss how to augment synthetic data in the next section. The same process can be used for real collected data also.

Augmenting Synthetic Data

In practice, there can be scenarios when human demonstration data is expensive. In our case with mountain car, while it is not expensive, it can be laborious. In either of these reasons, it is common practice to augment synthetic data to the expert human demonstrations. For training machine learning models, large and diverse datasets are beneficial. The expert trajectories collected and saved in previous section, can be augmented with synthetic data.A script augment_expert_data.py in the github repository provides a variety of functions to generate synthetic trajectories. The human trajectories can be used to create similar trajectories by perturbing the actions. This can be done by adding a random normally distributed noise to the human action, stepping through the environment loop, and collecting resulting state-action trajectories, as before.

def perturb_expert_actions(env, expert_data, noise_level=0.1, num_episodes=100):
    augmented_episodes = []
    for _ in range(num_episodes):
        state,_ = env.reset()  # Start from the initial state of the environment
        new_episode = []
        # Iterate over expert data and perturb actions
        for entry in expert_data:
            # Perturb the action with noise
            perturbed_action = entry['action'] + np.random.normal(0, noise_level)
            perturbed_action = np.clip(perturbed_action, -1.0, 1.0)  # Ensure within action bounds
            # Step the environment with the perturbed action to get the next state
            next_state, reward, done, _ , _= env.step([perturbed_action])
            # Create a new entry for the new episode
            new_entry = {
                'state': state.tolist(),
                'action': float(perturbed_action),
                'next_state': next_state.tolist(),
                'reward': reward,
                'done': done
            }
            new_episode.append(new_entry)
            # Update state for the next iteration
            state = next_state
            if done:
                break
        # Append the new episode to the list of augmented episodes
        augmented_episodes.append(new_episode)
    return augmented_episodes

Note that noise level can be varied to generate more diverse actions, but care should be taken to not introduce too much noise. In the next section we will use this generated data to train a behavioral cloning based model

Behavioral Cloning

There are several ways to incorporate human expert demonstration in training to improve PPO training performance. Collecting a similarity score between expert action and agent action and adding it to environmental reward, effectively reshaping the reward, can provide some immediate reward feedback instead of the delayed reward at the end of episode. While this is generally a valid approach, for sparse reward or delayed reward problems the agent might not encounter good sequence of actions to gain a high similarity score. A more robust approach is to train the agent to learn the human behavioral policy based on expert demonstrations and initialize the agent policy with learned behavioral policies before training on other exploratory samples. This ensures the agent has experience of good episodes. This is a direct policy initialization approach, and there can be some indirect approaches such as including human expert episodes in a replay buffer and using them as part of rollout episodes of PPO consistently.While we collected episodes with several variables in the last section, we only need states and actions for behavioral cloning. The behavioral cloning model training and inference are provided in the script expert_behavioral_cloning.py. This is a complete working code, but we will still cover important snippets here.Firstly, we create the behavioral cloning function, which requires inputting all training variables and setting up training. Note that device selection, agent assignment and optimizer setting is first executed. Expert trajectories are then unzipped to get states and actions, which are then converted to tensors.

def train_behavior_cloning(agent, expert_trajectories, num_epochs=10, batch_size=64, lr=0.001):
    device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() and args['cuda'] else "cpu")
    agent = agent.to(device)
    optimizer = optim.Adam(agent.parameters(), lr=lr, eps=1e-5)
    # Prepare data
    states, actions = zip(*expert_trajectories)  # Unzip into states and actions
    states = torch.tensor(states, dtype=torch.float32)
    actions = torch.tensor(actions, dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(1)

The training loop is then started for pre-decided number of epochs and number of steps. The states and actions are batched after randomized selection of indices.

# Training loop
    for epoch in range(num_epochs):
        perm = torch.randperm(states.size(0))
        for i in range(0, states.size(0), batch_size):
            indices = perm[i:i + batch_size]
            batch_states = states[indices]
            batch_actions = actions[indices]

This is followed by going through forward pass of the agent model. Note that we are not emphasizing changing anything with the agent model. We are only updating how we compute actions and log probabilities.

# Forward pass
            action_means = agent.actor_mean(batch_states)
            if agent.is_discrete:
                action_probs = nn.functional.softmax(action_means, dim=-1)
                log_probs = action_probs.log()[range(batch_actions.size(0)), batch_actions]
            else:
                action_logstd = agent.actor_logstd.expand_as(action_means)
                action_std = torch.exp(action_logstd)
                # Calculate the log probabilities for continuous actions
                dist = Normal(action_means, action_std)
                log_probs = dist.log_prob(batch_actions).sum(dim=1)

We then, compute losses. Here negative log probability is used for computing loss, which is standard. This is followed by backward pass and optimization. Note that we add gradient normalization to stabilize training.

 # Compute loss (negative log likelihood)
            loss = -log_probs.mean()
            # loss = nn.functional.mse_loss(action_means, batch_actions)
            # Backward pass and optimization
            optimizer.zero_grad()
            loss.backward()
            nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(agent.parameters(), max_norm=1.0)
            optimizer.step()

We add script to allow testing of the trained model and inferencing using gym environment, like previous PPO implementation. Here is the execution code for training and inference.

if __name__=='__main__':
    # Load config from YAML
    with open("args.yaml", "r") as f:
        args = yaml.safe_load(f)
    # Check if running inference
    run_inference = True
    # Setup environment
    device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() and args.cuda else "cpu")
    run_name = f"{args['env_id']}__{args['exp_name']}__{args['seed']}__{int(time.time())}"# # Save the model
    checkpoint_path = f'checkpoints/bc_checkpoint_{1}.pth'
    if not run_inference:
        envs = gym.vector.SyncVectorEnv([make_env(args['env_id'], i, args['capture_video'], run_name, args['gamma']) for i in range(args['num_envs'])])
        # Initialize agent
        agent = Agent(envs).to(device)
        with open('perturbed_expert_trajectories.json', 'r') as f:
            expert_episodes = json.load(f)
        states, actions = extract_expert_data(expert_episodes)
        train_behavior_cloning(agent, list(zip(states, actions)), num_epochs=50, batch_size=64,lr=1e-5)
        save_model(agent, checkpoint_path)
        envs.close()
    else:
        # Test on a small subset of expert trajectories
        test_behavior_cloning(agent, expert_episodes[:5])  # Testing on the first 5 expert trajectories
        # Run inference
        env = gym.make(args['env_id'], render_mode="human")
        # Initialize agent
        agent = Agent(env).to(device) 
        # Load the model from checkpoint
        load_model(agent, checkpoint_path)
        print("Starting Inference...")
        inference(env, agent, device, episodes=20, render=True)
        env.close()

Now that we covered behavioral cloning, we will integrate it with PPO and use it for model initialization to improve exploration.

Integration with PPO and Model Initialization

You will find that the trained behavioral cloning model based on human expert trajectories is successful in inference. This is expected for a problem like mountain car with limited stochasticity in states, apart from the amount introduced during simulation settings. Since Agent model is exactly same that was used for PPO, we initialize the agent before starting PPO training. This gives the model a headstart with good initial policy. It also makes PPO training more of a fine-tuning on the trained behavioral cloning based policy.The only change we have to make in previous PPO training script is to load the checkpoint saved for behavioral cloning based training, and add a conditional statement:

if args['bc_init']:
        load_model(agent, checkpoint_dir+f'/bc_checkpoint_{1}.pth')
        print("Behavioral Cloning Initialized")
        global_step = 0
        start_iteration = 0
    else:
        # Load the latest checkpoint if it exists
        latest_checkpoint = get_latest_checkpoint(checkpoint_dir)
            …

Then, we run the PPO training again. The training graphs show that episode length and returns are already high. So, PPO is getting a head-start. But, we may not see further improvement in performance, as for mountain car, we already get high expected returns:

In real-world problems where human demonstrations are themselves sub-optimal, adding behavioral cloning initialization gives a good starting point, an PPO training further improves over this initial policy. The policy initialization using human demonstrations also prohibits the PPO training from taking potentially dangerous or unnecessary actions, and keeps the policy at least at par with human performance level.

Summary

In this chapter, we discussed several concepts related to policy training. We started with discussing what involves policy training, limitations of q learning for scenarios when continuous systems are involved. Then we showed how some of the limitations of q-learning can be overcome by using a deep Q network (DQN), and demonstrated using it on a mountain car gym environment with discrete actions. We discussed limitations of DQN, such as inapplicability to systems with continuous actions and stability issues.Then, we focused on policy gradient algorithms, and how they evolved from vanilla policy gradients to advantage actor-critic, to generalized advantage estimation algorithm, and how the problem of variability is mitigated by subtracting value functions from returns. We then discuss concepts of proximal policy gradients and key ideas of clipping mechanism and containing policy step to within a threshold based on metrics such as KL divergence. Then, we discussed the implementation of continuous mountain car problem, a highly sparse reward problem requiring a lot of exploration.Then, we discussed how human feedback in the form of human demonstrations can be used to improve PPO training time. For this we collected a handful of human demonstrations, augmented it with synthetic data, and used it for training a behavioral cloning model. This model then initialized the PPO training, which essentially acts as fine-tuning mechanism to the behavioral cloning model.In this chapter, we focused on policy training algorithms, their types, limitations, and evolution into state-of-the-art. By studying continuous mountain car, and the sparse reward problems, we understand that reward modeling might not be sufficient, as the agent would see good reward sporadically after a large amount of training. For such cases, using behavioral cloning based on human demonstrations and initializing policy for training using RL algorithms such as PPO can be more beneficial. We also showed mechanism of initializing the PPO agent model using pretrained behavioral cloning based model. With the fundamental understanding of RL and Human Feedback methods, now you are ready to undertake discussing applications of RL such as using RLHF for fine-tuning Language Models, which is part of the next section of the book.

Chapter 5: Introduction to Language Models and Fine Tuning

Join our book community on Discord

https://packt.link/EarlyAccessCommunity

As we transition into this chapter on language modeling and fine-tuning, we will explore advanced concepts critical for understanding large language models (LLMs) in natural language processing (NLP). While previous chapters covered fundamentals of reinforcement learning (RL), reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), reward modeling and policy optimizaiton, including basic deep neural networks, this chapter will advance to more sophisticated architectures, including the transformer.This chapter includes the following topics:

  • Introduction to Language Models
  • The Transformer Architecture
  • Fine-Tuning Language Models
  • RLHF in Fine-Tuning
  • Evaluation and Challenges in Fine-Tuning with RLHF

You will gain insights into the fundamentals and history of language models, learn about the transformative impact of transformers, and discover how to fine-tune pretrained models for specific tasks. We will also discuss how RLHF aligns models with human intent and cover evaluation methods and ethical considerations for fine-tuning with RLHF, setting the stage for future developments in the field.

Technical requirements

For this chapter, we have kept all implementation related dependencies minimal. All implementation is in python. Specific version recommendations and the minimal required libraries that need to be installed are:

  • Python version >= 3.9
  • numpy == 1.26.4
  • matplotlib == 3.8.4
  • transformers == 4.42.4
