Chapter 6: Parameter Efficient Fine Tuning

Join our book community on Discord

https://packt.link/EarlyAccessCommunity

In the previous chapter we introduced transformer model encoder-decoder architecture, and how to use transformers library and pretrained models to fine-tune on specific datasets. Here, we will further discuss the approach of parameter efficient fine tuning, introduce the Transformer Reinforcement Learning (TRL) library for explaining Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) process, discuss in details various techniques such as low-rank adaptor for fine tuning, evaluation of Parameter Efficient Fine Tuning (PEFT) models and the workflow.Fine-tuning a large pretrained model involves updating the weights of the model to refine its policy for performing specific tasks. The number of parameters to update is large, making the fine-tuning process computationally expensive. In this chapter, you will learn how to efficiently fine-tune models by updating only the essential parameters and thereby saving significant costs. For practical consideration, this makes the chapter important.This chapter will cover the following topics:

  • Introduction to Parameter Efficient Fine-Tuning
  • Applying Supervised Fine-Tuning Process Using Hugging Face TRL Library
  • Understanding Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) Technique
  • Evaluating PEFT Models
  • Fine-Tuning and Evaluation Workflow

Technical requirements

For this chapter, we have kept all implementation related dependencies minimal. All implementation is in python. Specific version recommendations and the minimal required libraries that need to be installed are:

  • Python version >= 3.9
  • numpy == 1.26.4
  • matplotlib == 3.8.4
  • transformers == 4.42.4
  • datasets == 3.0.1
  • ipython == 8.27.0
  • ipywidgets == 8.1.5

In addition to the above libraries and installation, we will also use google colab for fine-tuning using GPUs (more on that later).To get started, clone this git repository: https://github.com/PacktPublishing/A-Practical-Guide-to-Reinforcement-Learning-from-Human-Feedback, and change directory to chapter 6.

Introduction to Parameter Efficient Fine Tuning

In fine-tuning, a pre-trained model such as a large transformer is further trained on a small, task-specific dataset. The additional training on specific dataset, allows the model to learn the task specifics and incorporate new knowledge in the dataset. In the previous chapter, we demonstrated fine-tuning the `distilbert-base-uncased` model to the imdb dataset. This model was relatively smaller (66 million parameters) compared to contemporary models (in order of billions) and allowed demonstration on a small dataset.Traditional fine-tuning saves considerable training cost by training on a task specific smaller dataset. It still usually requires updating a large number of parameters, which can be computationally expensive and memory-intensive. To address this, a class of fine-tuning techniques called parameter efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) has become a common practice.The goal of PEFT strategies is to reduce the number of parameters to fine-tune, and this can be achieved through several methods. Broadly, we can classify them as follows:Additive Fine-Tuning Methods: In this subclass of fine-tuning, the original pretrained model parameters are frozen, and instead, added parameters are augmented and trained. This fundamentally changes the structure of the model. These methods can use adapters—small neural networks augmented with the pre-trained model. This significantly reduces the number of parameters that need to be adjusted.Prompt Based Methods: Prominent among these is prompt tuning —instead of modifying the model directly, this approach involves learning a set of input prompts that help in conditioning the model output. These prompts are short enough for practical purposes and can capture the desired behavior without any change to the model architecture. These methods use trainable embeddings that prepend or append to the inputs, and guide the model output, while the pretrained model weights are unaltered. This method has shown promise in small data settings and model sizes, but for larger models there are diminishing returns.Specification Based Methods: These methods involve specifying model parameters for tuning, either heuristically (randomly or manually) or by learning them. While these methods have been applied in research projects, they are not shown much promise in practice for large language model adaption.Reparameterization based methods: This method is based on the premise that pretrained model parameters can be adapted for specific tasks by and representing them with a lower rank. This compression to low-dimensional space can be an optimization that leads to lower computation cost. Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) introduces low-rank matrices into the model architecture, updating only these matrices during training. This reduces the number of trainable parameters significantly and also enables effective adaptation to training datasets. In LoRA, weight updates involve factorizing the model parameters into low-rank matrices. Training only these matrices provides an efficient adaptation mechanism for the pretrained models, compared to updating all of the weights in case of full fine-tuning.Among these approaches, LoRA is the state of the art technique, and often utilized in research papers as a benchmark technique to compare to. Therefore, we will focus on this technique in more depth, in this chapter. Before going through this technique, we will first investigate the Supervised Fine-Tuning process, which specializes in fine-tuning for language models.

Applying Supervised Fine-Tuning Process Using Hugging Face TRL Library

Fine-tuning using the standard fine-tuning we demonstrated in Chapter 5 can become more complex as we require making several training choices. The transformers library allows flexibility to make these choices, but it comes at the cost of longer development time and experimentation. It also requires several customizations and developments to integrate methods necessary for implementing RLHF. To address these challenges, the TRL library dedicates to providing tools and techniques that help the development of post-training model processes. The modular architecture also helps with scaling to various hardware setups.Supervised Fine Tuning (SFT) method is a modality within the TRL library, and it is similar to the standard fine-tuning as it is a wrapper around the trainer class of Transformer library, and both involve adapting a pre-trained model to a specific tasks. The main difference between them is that SFT is specifically designed for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) and provides tools and features that facilitate training on labeled datasets with prompt-response pairs, tailored for conversational tasks, and allows further model specification and initialization. On the contrary, the general fine-tuning functionality in Transformers, while more flexible for a wider range of tasks, may require additional dataset formatting and customization when used for LLM specific applications, especially around conversational use cases.SFT provides features such as prompt engineering and dataset formatting and relies a lot on prompt-response pairs in the training data. This allows the model to learn to generate outputs and responses based on specific prompts and formats. TRL offers a dedicated SFTTrainer class that simplifies the process of supervised fine-tuning with features like dataset handling and LLM evaluation metrics. In this section we will use the SFTTrainer and SFTConfigs from the TRL library, for fine-tuning a model, in place of TrainingArguments and Trainer from the transformers library that we used in the last chapter. The SFTConfig helps configure aspects of the fine-tuning process, and also helps setting up the reward model and other parameters specific to SFT in reinforcement learning-based fine-tuning setups.

Model Choice

SFTTrainer is a specialized class developed for causal language modeling. These models predict the next word based on a given input of words or tokens. Some applications include text completion, auto-correction and code generation. These models are inherently different than masked language models, such as BERT (bidirectional encoder representations for transformers) and distilBERT (a distilled version of BERT), that we discussed in the last chapter. Due to architectural differences, SFTTrainer is suitable for causal language modeling especially large language models such as GPT series. For masked models, transformers library should be used.

Fine Tuning using SFT Trainer

SFTTrainer fine-tunes the causal models, and you can realize that by going through the snippets in this section. Since SFTTrainer can be used for fine-tuning on models which are reasonably large, in the order of several millions to billions, SFT without PEFT can be expensive. So care has to be taken to not run models indefinitely.A simple fine-tuning process can look something like this. First install the required libraries in the colab notebook:

! pip install transformers datasets trl torch

Import required libraries as below. Note that we are using some of the classes from transformers library, such as the AutoTokenizer and AutoModelForSequenceClassification. These help maintain the same workflow. In addition to these, we are using the SFTtrainer and SFTConfig classes from the TRL library.

import numpy as np
from datasets import load_dataset
from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForSequenceClassification
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score, f1_score
from trl import SFTTrainer, SFTConfig

Load the sample training and test datasets. In real applications, these will be large datasets, but for purpose of demonstration, we are loading only a small number of samples.

train_dataset = load_dataset("imdb", split="train[:4%]")
eval_dataset = load_dataset("imdb", split="train[4%:5%]")
print(f"Train Data:{train_dataset}")
print(f"Eval Data:{eval_dataset}")

We will use a custom evaluation function that can be passed to SFTTrainer. The accuracy_score and f1_score metrics are imported from sklearn library.

# Evaluation function
def compute_metrics(pred):
    logits, labels = pred
    predictions = np.argmax(logits, axis=-1)
    accuracy = accuracy_score(labels, predictions)
    f1 = f1_score(labels, predictions, average="weighted")
    return {"accuracy": accuracy, "f1": f1}

SFTTrainer and SFTConfig can be implemented as below. This involves preparing configs and passing to the trainer. Note that we are using a specific model for fine-tuning—facebook/opt-350m. It is a model developed by Meta (formerly Facebook), as part of the OPT (Open Pretrained Transformer) series. It’s a language model developed to demonstrate high-performance capabilities in various natural language processing (NLP) tasks such as text generation, completion, summarization and translation, as well as sentiment analysis. This is a good model to experiment with since it is one of the smaller models with 350 million parameters, yet useful for NLP tasks.

training_args = SFTConfig(
    max_seq_length=512,
    output_dir="/tmp",
    report_to= 'none'
)
trainer = SFTTrainer(
    "facebook/opt-350m",
    train_dataset=train_dataset,
    eval_dataset=eval_dataset,
    args=training_args,
    compute_metrics=compute_metrics
)
trainer.train()

After training, the fine-tuned model can be evaluated using the evaluate method of the trainer.

# Evaluation after fine-tuning
fine_tuned_results = trainer.evaluate()
print("Results after Fine-Tuning:", fine_tuned_results)

To try the SFT training process, you can refer to the notebook on the github link for the book, under this chapter. You must now realize that the fine-tuning process is significantly simplified by TRL library. You may experiment with other causal models or GPT models, but note that facebook/opt-350m is relatively less resource-intensive, and more resources might be needed to complete fine-tuning. In the next section, we will explore how we can be more efficient with fine-tuning, using the state-of-the-art technique, low-rank adaptation (LoRA), and apply it for fine-tuning gpt-2—a model often used in research, development, and experimentation.

Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) Technique

The basic idea behind Low-rank Adaptation (LoRA) technique is to fine-tune only a small number of model weights relevant for steering output to something closer to defined dataset for fine-tuning, instead of training the entire set of model weights. This reduction in training parameters leads to reduced memory requirements and total training cost. The small number of model weights are a decomposition of model weights update.A typical model fine-tuning of models we are discussing is in order of several millions to several billions. Using LoRA, the weight matrices are decomposed to low rank matrices, and training only these low rank matrices. Let us consider as the weight of the fine-tuned model, and W as the weight of pretrained model that needs to be fine-tuned. The update applied to the weight matrix can be .Here, LoRA introduces two low-rank matrices A and B, where the rank is much smaller than the original matrix.

LoRA using PEFT library

We will first show how LoRA can be implemented using the methods from the parameter efficient fine tuning library from HuggingFace. This library allows us to finetune models by first creating a low-rank adapter.In order to apply LoRA, we first install all the required libraries. Note that we are adding peft to previous installations.

! pip install transformers datasets trl torch peft

Some of the methods from transformers library are imported as they can be used as-is for implementing fine-tuning. This helps augmenting fine-tuning with parameter efficient methods directly. The classes from transformers library are directly utilized for parts of the pipeline development. For example, AutoTokenizer class automatically loads a tokenizer based on a model identifier. Specifying the pre-trained model, helps in transforming text data into a format that the model can process. This also ensures consistency between training and inference. Similarly, the AutoModelForCausalLM class loads a pre-trained language model for causal language modeling tasks, which are necessary for predicting the next token in a sequence, making it immediately applicable for autoregressive models like GPT that often involve text generation. Once the model identifier is specified, the corresponding pretrained model is loaded.Using TrainingArguments and Trainer class from transformers library and augmenting with the PEFT library specific classes helps with extensibility of functionalities in future. The primary reason to do this is that transformers library is the core library on top of which several other applications and libraries will continue to be developed. Using classes from transformers makes integration with these other applications along with PEFT more likely to be compatible. TrainingArguments class can be used for configuring more basic training parameters such as learning rate, batch size, number of epochs, and logging details. Instantiating TrainingArguments for defining how the training loop should behave by setting parameters for optimizer, steps for evaluation, gradient accumulation, and importing classes from PEFT library for setting parameters specific to those configs. The following snippet shows essential imported classes.

from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM, TrainingArguments, Trainer
from datasets import Dataset
from peft import get_peft_model, LoraConfig, TaskType

The peft library is a relatively new in the huggingface ecosystem and specifically designed for making fine-tuning process of large language models more parameter-efficient. It incorporates methods such as LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) to modify models in a more resource-efficient manner. It has methods such as get_peft_model function, which is used to apply parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques (like LoRA) to a model. The user has to only pass a pre-trained model to this function, relevant fine-tuning technique and corresponding configuration parameters, and it applies the specified fine-tuning technique, returning the modified model. The configuration parameters, in this case for LoRA, can be specified using the LoraConfig class. Using LoraConfig, you can define the specifics of how you want to apply LoRA to your model. These can be rank size, layers to apply, and target modules. In the below example,

lora_config = LoraConfig(r=16, lora_alpha=32, target_modules=["attn.query", "attn.value"])

the r parameter controls the rank of the low-rank approximation, and lora_alpha controls the scaling of the updates.Fine-tuning can be applied for various tasks, and the TaskType class can help define the type of task the model is to be adapted for e.g. for text generation or classification. It therefore keeps the model architecture and fine-tuning process consistent with the target task specified. This can be simply applied as: task_type = TaskType.CAUSAL_LM. Implementing fine-tuning with LoRA involves following steps:

  1. Load Pre-trained Model and Tokenizer:

Specify the pre-trained model to be fine-tuned e.g. gpt2, depending on where it needs to be used. Pass the model name to tokenizer and the automodel for causal language model, pad the tokenizer.

model_name = "gpt2"
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(model_name)
  1. Define PEFT Configuration (LoRA)

Then, define the PEFT configuration for LoRA. The configuration can include, task type, rank of LoRA matrices, alpha and dropout of LoRA, and the target modules where it is to be inserted.

peft_config = LoraConfig(
    task_type=TaskType.CAUSAL_LM,
    r=8,          # Rank of LoRA matrices
    lora_alpha=32,
    lora_dropout=0.1,
    target_modules=["c_attn"],  # Adjusted for GPT-2
)

Then, pass the configs and model to which the LoRA is to be be applied.

 model = get_peft_model(model, peft_config)
  1. Prepare Custom Dataset

Now, that the model is configured and structured, prepare custom dataset, or use one of the opensource datasets. Here we will use a custom dataset and pass the dictionary to the method from Dataset class.

data = {"text": ["I love AI because", "ChatGPT helps with", "AI will revolutionize"]}
dataset = Dataset.from_dict(data)

We can write a tokenization function to specify padding and add labels for loss computations. The tokenize_function maps the dataset text to the numerics as tokens and corresponding ids as labels.

def tokenize_function(example):
    tokenized = tokenizer(example["text"], padding="max_length", truncation=True, max_length=32)
    tokenized["labels"] = tokenized["input_ids"].copy()
    return tokenized

To do this mapping, the map method of dataset is used.

tokenized_dataset = dataset.map(tokenize_function, batched=True)
  1. Define Training Arguments

At the same time, along with tokenized dataset, we can configure the training arguments. Here, we can define output directory, training batch size, number of epochs, loggings, and saving strategy. Training parameters such as learning rate for training speed and accuracy and mixed precision setting for memory optimization e.g. fp16, can also be set here. The `report_to` config is usually for specifying whether we want to plot data or record it for analysis using experimentation frameworks such as Weights and Biases. In this below snippet, we are just using ‘none’.

training_args = TrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./results",
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    num_train_epochs=3,
    logging_dir="./logs",
    save_strategy="no", 
    learning_rate=2e-4,
    fp16=True,
    report_to= 'none',
)
  1. Fine-Tune the Model

After defining and setting up all the configs and the model, we can pass them to the Trainer method and train the model.

trainer = Trainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=tokenized_dataset,
)
trainer.train()

Upon training completion, you have your pretrained model fine-tuned. You now understand how to fine-tune a pretrained model using PEFT library. For detailed implementation, you can check the notebook in the github repository. The other approach of efficient fine-tuning using TRL library is using the SFT trainer, which we will continue to explore in the next.

LoRA using SFT Trainer

Now, that we know how to use PEFT library and apply LoRA for fine-tuning, we will discuss how to use SFT Trainer to apply LoRA, as this is how it will be integrated with RLHF based fine-tuning also.SFT Trainer relies on PEFT library also for integrating the PEFT configs into the training. In the previous section on Supervised Fine-Tuning Process Using Hugging Face TRL Library, we used training_args and passed it to SFT Trainer. Using PEFT and LoRA also include peft_config to SFT Trainer. When using PEFT library directly, we get the augmented model by using get_model method from PEFT. But, while using SFT Trainer for fine-tuning using LoRA, we don’t have to do this step. We directly pass peft_config to the SFT Trainer. The model used is the pretrained model retrieved from AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(model_name). A snippet example is given next.

from trl import SFTConfig, SFTTrainer
trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model,
    train_dataset=tokenized_dataset2,
    args=SFTConfig(output_dir="/tmp",report_to= 'none'),
    peft_config=peft_config,
)
trainer.train()

At this point, you know how to use SFT for fine-tuning as well as how to apply LoRA using SFT and train (fine-tune) the pre-trained models, in addition to using PEFT library. After fine-tuning the models, it is necessary to evaluate the models, and once you have satisfactory model responses, it is ready for inference. We will discuss this next.

Inference and Evaluate PEFT Models

After training, using the eval method, the trained model can be evaluated. Test using some input_text , tokenize it using the tokenizer function. Note that you may get a warning if the attention mask and the pad token id are not set. You need to explicitly provide attention_mask along with the input IDs during inference. Additionally, you should set pad_token_id explicitly for text generation.

model.eval()
input_text = "AI is transforming"
inputs = tokenizer(input_text, return_tensors="pt", padding=True).to("cuda")
output = model.generate(
    inputs["input_ids"],
    attention_mask=inputs["attention_mask"], 
    max_length=50,
    num_return_sequences=1,
    pad_token_id=tokenizer.eos_token_
)
print(tokenizer.decode(output[0],skip_special_tokens=True))

An exercise for users can be using two datasets in step 3 above, repeating steps 4 and 5, fine-tuning using the two datasets, and comparing model generation. The two datasets should have a significant difference in data, such that the first dataset is more general and second dataset is more domain specific. Here is an example showing a sample dataset. Note that you will have to generate more sentences than the number of sentences used in these examples.Dataset 1 (General):

data_1 =  {
    "text": [
        "Artificial intelligence is revolutionizing many industries by automating tasks and improving decision-making.",
        "Machine learning models are used in applications like predictive analytics and natural language processing.",
        "AI can assist in various sectors such as healthcare, finance, and transportation by analyzing large datasets.",
        "Deep learning techniques are powering advancements in computer vision, enabling machines to interpret images and video.",
        "AI technologies like robotics are being deployed to perform tasks that were once considered too complex for machines."
    ]
}

Dataset 2 (domain specific):

data_2 = {
    "text": [
        "AI is transforming oncology by helping in the early detection of cancer through advanced imaging techniques.",
        "Machine learning algorithms are increasingly used to analyze tumor biopsies and predict cancer progression.",
        "In precision oncology, AI is utilized to create personalized treatment plans based on a patient's genetic profile.",
        "AI-driven systems are improving the accuracy of radiology by identifying tumors in medical scans like CT and MRI images.",
        "Artificial intelligence is accelerating drug discovery, identifying potential cancer therapies faster than traditional methods."
    ]
}

After comparing the outputs, you can notice how the output is modified for the model fine-tuned with domain specific dataset.The model generation and inferencing involve significant amount of experimentation using additional parameter setting. For example, we add temperature parameter to adjust temperature for controlled randomness (lower means less randomness). Similarly top_p is a parameter to increase diverse but focused output. top_k parameter sets the sampling limits on the pool of options for better quality. no_repeat_ngram_size prevents repeating n-grams, otherwise word repetition can be observed, and do_sample setting is to enable more creative answers. Also, note that we are now not outputting only the first generated response like in previous model generation (output[0], but returning outputs enumerated outputs, which are equal to setting num_return_sequences.

model.eval()
input_text = "AI in oncology"
inputs = tokenizer(input_text, return_tensors="pt", padding=True).to("cuda")
# Explicitly set pad_token_id
# Set parameters to influence generation quality
output = model.generate(
    inputs["input_ids"],
    attention_mask=inputs["attention_mask"], 
    max_length=100, 
    num_return_sequences=2, 
    pad_token_id=tokenizer.eos_token_id, 
    temperature=0.7, 
    top_p=0.9, 
    top_k=50,
    no_repeat_ngram_size=2, 
    do_sample=True, 
)
print("Generated Responses:")
for i, generated_sequence in enumerate(output):
    print(f"Option {i+1}: {tokenizer.decode(generated_sequence, skip_special_tokens=True)}\n")

In addition to model generation setting experiments, users can do training experiments by training multiple fine-tuned models by changing the LoRA parameters:

r=8,         
lora_alpha=32,
lora_dropout=0.1,
target_modules=["c_attn"]

Note that a higher value of r, means more model expressivity but lower computational efficiency. The scaling factor lora_alpha dictates how strongly the model will adapt to task specific data used in fine-tuning. The value lora_dropout decides percentage of the low-rank parameters that will be dropped out during training. This helps to improve generalization and prevents the model from relying heavily on specific patterns learned using the low-rank matrices. target_modules specify which parts of the model will be adapted using LoRA. By specifying c_attn we are specifying LoRA to focus the low-rank adaptation on the attention mechanisms of the model. This is fine as attention layers help the model understand dependencies in sequences of text.In addition to manual evaluation using model inferencing, for deploying fine-tuned models is production, they have to be thoroughly evaluated using both qualitative and quantitative metrics. Fine-tuning of pretrained foundation models usually is based on what tasks the model needs to serve e.g. model completion, text generation, sentiment analysis or summarization. In order to evaluate fine-tuned models, evaluation datasets can be prepared based on fine-tuning dataset used. These datasets can then be used to test the model outputs on metrics that are relevant to the tasks. For example, tasks such as summarization, can be evaluated using metrics such as recall – this metric indicates the ratio of relevant results retrieved or generated in the output from the model, for total number of relevant results available in the fine-tuning dataset, and precision—the number of relevant output n-grams that are correctly available in the fine-tuning dataset. These metrics can be captured in variations of the well-known metric called Recall-Oriented Understudy for Gisting Evaluation (ROUGE). For example ROUGE-n is a metric that indicates the number of overlaps between the number of n-grams available in the dataset used in fine-tuning and number of n-grams in the generated outputs. ROUGE-1 for unigram overlaps involving measuring overlaps in single word, and ROUGE-2 for bi-grams for measuring overlaps of two words at a time are also used sometimes.These statistical metrics only capture partial quality of the output, for example, while ROUGE captures overlap, it does not capture fluency of the generated output. These metrics also do not capture bias or other important metrics. Therefore, in addition to statistical metrics, we rely on model based metrics, where the LLM model can judge itself or a more advanced LLM judges the outputs for all required criteria.For this chapter, the focus is mainly on reducing model update cost and improving training efficiency, while maintaining performance on task inference. Model evaluation process for parameter-efficient fine-tuning, supervised fine-tuning or RLHF will be similar. We will defer this topic and elaborately treat it in Chapter 11.

Summary

In this chapter, we discussed challenges of fine-tuning, and how fine-tuning can become memory intensive as the pretrained models start getting larger, especially in case of large foundational models. In order to address this problem, you are introduced to PEFT, and guided through the process of how it can be applied hands-on using the PEFT library from HuggingFace. In this chapter, you learned various methods of fine-tuning, along with their benefits and overheads. Developing familiarity of how Supervised Fine-Tuning method from the TRL library could integrate PEFT as discussed in this chapter, will help in later chapters, as we can integrate them for applying RLHF from TRL, and is useful, in general, for any fine-tuning based method. Similarly, after studying this chapter, you should have a better understanding of one of the predominant PEFT techniques, Low Rank Adapter (LoRA), how to apply it for a real-world problem by going through all steps of the process. We also covered how you can infer the fine-tuned model for tasks like text completion and generation, which will provide you with good starting point to build a fine-tuning pipeline. In the following chapter, we will start discussing how to develop a reward model based on human preference data that can help provide reward signals for tuning and aligning models to human preferences.

Chapter 7: Reward Modeling for Language Model Tuning

Join our book community on Discord

In the previous chapter we introduced parameter efficient fine tuning (PEFT) techniques, such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), and demonstrated how the trl library can be used to efficiently fine-tune (FT) large language models (LLMs) through Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT). While fine-tuning helps align a pretrained model with specific datasets or downstream tasks, it often falls short in aligning the model’s behavior with nuanced human preferences. Therefore, SFT is typically regarded as the first phase in the Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) pipeline.To bridge the gap between task performance and human-aligned behavior, RLHF introduces two key stages beyond SFT:Reward Modeling – learning a reward function based on human preferences.Policy Optimization – using the reward model to fine-tune the model via reinforcement learning.This chapter focuses on the first of these: the Reward Modeling process. We will explore how human preferences are collected, how they are used to train a reward model, and how this model enables better alignment of language model outputs with human expectations. and discuss the steps required for building a reward model.Key topics include:

  • Introduction to Reward Modeling for Language
  • Data Preprocessing and Preference Data Collection
  • Exploring Techniques for Reward Modeling
  • Model Evaluation and Iteration

Overview of Reward Modeling for Language

Large Language Models (LLMs) are typically pretrained on large-scale corpora using supervised learning objectives such as cross-entropy loss, which optimizes the model to accurately predict the next token. This pretraining enables fluent and syntactically correct generation, but it does not guarantee that the outputs are factually accurate, helpful, or aligned with user intent. While this allows the model to generate fluent and syntactically correct text, it does not guarantee that the outputs are factually accurate, helpful, or aligned with user intent. This disconnect often results in phenomena such as hallucination, where the model produces confident sounding but incorrect, unhelpful or misleading information.To address such shortcomings, SFT is commonly employed to adapt models to task-specific datasets. While SFT improves task relevance and reduces some undesirable behaviors, it remains limited by the static nature of the training data and does not incorporate subjective human preferences. For instance, given two plausible responses, SFT offers no mechanism to indicate which is more helpful or aligned with human values.Reward modeling (RM) addresses this limitation by learning a function that maps model outputs to scalar reward values, based on human feedback. The most common approach involves collecting comparison data, where annotators evaluate pairs of model responses to the same prompt and select the preferred one. These comparisons are used to train a reward model capable of generalizing human preferences to unseen outputs.In the context of RLHF, the RM acts as a surrogate reward function to guide policy optimization. Typically, RM and the reinforcement learning (RL) agent are trained sequentially. First, the RM is trained on human preference data—usually in the form of output comparisons or ratings. Then, this fixed RM is used during RL to provide reward signals. Although online RM updates are occasionally explored in experimental settings, they are rare and require careful handling to maintain training stability. The modular, sequential nature of this pipeline makes it easier to maintain and iterate on the reward model as an independent subsystem.In the context of RLHF, the RM acts as a surrogate reward function to guide policy optimization. Typically, the reward model (RM) and the reinforcement learning (RL) agent are not trained simultaneously, but rather sequentially. First, the RM is trained on human preference data —usually in the form of output comparisons or ratings. Then, this fixed RM is used during RL to provide reward signals. The modular, sequential structure of the RLHF pipeline remains a practical and widely adopted approach, as it enables easier maintenance, debugging, and independent iteration on the reward model.Emerging exceptions to fixed RM practice include systems like Online Iterative RLHF: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2405.07863 , which employ true online reward modeling: the RM is refined during policy optimization using proxy human feedback (e.g., GPT-4 annotations) from newly sampled rollouts. Hybrid approaches, such as HIRE (Hybrid Intrinsic Reward: https://arxiv.org/html/2501.12627v1 ) or RTV-GenRM: https://arxiv.org/html/2503.22230v1 , combine static preference-based rewards with dynamic components (e.g., curiosity-driven exploration or reasoning verifiers) to balance alignment and adaptability. While these advanced methods show promise, the sequential workflow remains dominant in production systems due to its simplicity, scalability, and acceptable resource feasibility.Preference-based RM is the dominant approach in RLHF for language models, particularly due to the open-ended nature of language generation, where multiple valid outputs may exist. In such cases, direct comparisons or scalar ratings from humans help resolve ambiguity more effectively than demonstration-based methods such as imitation learning, behavioral cloning, or inverse reinforcement learning. These alternative approaches focus on mimicking human behavior or inferring reward functions from demonstrations but are less suited to subjective tasks involving nuanced judgments.The reward modeling process typically involves three stages: (1) Data Collection—assembling input-output pairs and annotating them with human preference signals, such as binary comparisons (e.g., accepted vs. rejected responses) or numerical scores; (2) Data Preprocessing—structuring, cleaning, and validating the dataset to ensure quality and consistency; and (3) Model Training and Evaluation—training a neural network to predict reward values from the input-output pairs and evaluating its performance on benchmark datasets.Reward modeling for LLM fine-tuning can be implemented using custom pipelines or prebuilt libraries. In Chapter 3, we explored how to build a reward model in PyTorch and integrate it into an RL setup for tasks such as gridworld navigation. In Chapter 4, we implemented a custom PPO algorithm for policy training. While such custom implementations offer transparency and flexibility, they also introduce significant maintenance overhead, particularly given the rapid pace of updates in model architectures and infrastructure.Reward modeling for LLM fine-tuning can be implemented using custom pipelines or prebuilt libraries. In Chapter 3, we explored how to build a reward model in PyTorch and integrate it into an RL setup for tasks such as gridworld navigation. In Chapter 4, we implemented a custom PPO algorithm for policy training. While such custom implementations offer transparency and flexibility, they also introduce significant maintenance overhead, particularly given the rapid pace of updates in model architectures and infrastructure.To mitigate these challenges, standardized libraries such as Hugging Face’s TRL provide integrated support for RLHF workflows. TRL facilitates the use of pretrained transformer models (e.g., GPT, T5, BERT), supports reward modeling, and offers out-of-the-box implementations of policy optimization methods such as proximal policy optimization (PPO). It also enables experimentation with newer approaches like Direct Preference Optimization (DPO).For most practical use cases, the benefits of adopting a standardized RLHF stack—faster development, improved scalability, and long-term maintainability—generally outweigh the advantages of building and maintaining custom reward modeling infrastructure. In the next section, we examine the first step of reward modeling in detail: preference data collection and preprocessing.

Data Preprocessing and Preference Data Collection

For collecting human preference data, usually crowd workers are tasked with chat sessions for interacting with models and adding preferences. This involves firstly asking the model questions or discussing some topic inducing a conversation, and upon receiving response from the model, rating the model outputs or assigning a preference score. This can be an expensive process, requiring human workers to perform these tasks.The feedback or preference score can be inaccurate or biased, and based entirely on the crowd worker. Therefore, crowd worker curation becomes part of the data collection process, in addition to the data collection.

Raw data from collections

Raw data can be collected in a json format or any other form convenient for preprocessing. The data collection process typically involves presenting a large number of prompts and paired model responses to human annotators via a user interface instrumented with telemetry. Annotators are asked to express a preference—either by selecting the better of two responses or by rejecting both. This preference is then encoded using binary labels, such as 1 indicating that response_1 is preferred, and 0 indicating that response_2 is preferred. An example raw data is given below.

raw_data = [
    {
        "prompt": "Tell me a joke.",
        "response_1": "Why did the chicken cross the road?",
        "response_2": "Knock, knock!",
        "label": 1,  # 1 means response_1 is preferred
    },
    {
        "prompt": "What is AI?",
        "response_1": "Artificial Intelligence.",
        "response_2": "A type of plant.",
        "label": 0,  # 0 means response_2 is preferred
    },
]

The collected raw data can be large, and is usually stored in a database. Before RM training, this data is accessed and preprocessed. In practice, it is best to start with a json/csv on git, for starting out. This is especially suitable if update to the data is not frequent, e.g. daily. As the data size grows, or is estimated to grow, and there are scenarios of frequently changing datasets, and need for advanced querying, a SQL or NoSQL database is more appropriate.Regardless of storage format, curating high-quality data is essential for stable and accurate reward modeling. Some best practices to help ensure that preference datasets remain reliable are as follows:Annotator training: Providing clear labeling guidelines and example tasks to promote consistency can help improve the quality of raw data.Prompt diversity: Including a variety of task types can help avoid overfitting the reward model to a narrow domain.Response quality filtering: If both the responses in the pair are uninformative or too similar, it will not be helpful during training, and should be filtered out.Monitor agreement: Tracking inter-annotator agreement and refining prompt instructions based on review of disagreements can help with signal consistency.Bias auditing: Human data collection can lead to systematic preferences or cultural bias, and periodically reviewing the data to check bias, can help remove bias and save cost on de-biasing the data.Uncertainty signals: Human annotator may have varied confidence level while adding preference labels, and including a confidence level field can inform how those labels are weighted during training.Deduplication and variation: Duplicate examples should be avoided and stylistic diversity in model outputs should be encouraged, so that outputs reflect a range of tones, phrasings, and expressive styles. This is particularly useful when training reward models, since human preference is often sensitive not only to correctness or helpfulness, but also to how something is said.These steps help produce a dataset that not only captures human preferences faithfully but also supports robust and generalizable reward modeling.

Preprocess data

Once the raw data has been retrieved—whether from a database or a JSON file— we have to first preprocess it to use it into a format suitable for training. Typically, we begin by loading the raw list of prompt-response-label dictionaries into a pandas DataFrame. The goal of preprocessing is to map the human preference labels into a structured format that separates accepted and rejected responses into their own columns. This format is not only convenient but essential for many preference learning setups. In some preference models, “chosen” or “accepted” and “rejected” are distinct labels to model responses and clearly indicate what the human annotators chose.

df = pd.DataFrame(raw_data)
def map_responses(row):
    if row["label"] == 1:
        return {"prompt": row["prompt"], "accepted": row["response_1"], "rejected": row["response_2"]}
    else:
        return {"prompt": row["prompt"], "accepted": row["response_2"], "rejected": row["response_1"]}
processed_data = df.apply(map_responses, axis=1).to_list()
dataset = Dataset.from_list(processed_data)

Structuring the data with accepted and rejected columns serves an important purpose. In many reward modeling pipelines, the learning objective is based on pairwise comparisons, where the model is trained to assign a higher reward to the preferred response than to the rejected one. This data format aligns directly with such objectives. It is particularly helpful not just for neural reward models, but also for scoring models based on logistic preference modeling or Bradley-Terry-style ranking models, which rely explicitly on clear preferred vs. rejected pairs.By preprocessing the data in this way, we ensure compatibility across a range of reward modeling techniques while making the supervision signal explicit and interpretable.

Data Tokenization

After preprocessing the dataset to clearly separate the accepted and rejected responses for each prompt, the next step is to tokenize the text so it can be used for model training. Tokenization involves converting each text input into a sequence of token IDs, along with attention masks to indicate which tokens should be attended to by the model.We begin by selecting a base pretrained language model whose tokenizer we will use. For instance, we can use a BERT tokenizer model, such as bert-base-uncased, using the AutoTokenizer method in HuggingFace. This ties the tokenization process to the model architecture so that it can be used during model training. It can be declared as follows:

model_name = "bert-base-uncased"
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)

For preference data, we tokenize both the accepted and rejected responses alongside the prompt. This allows the reward model to later distinguish between high- and low-quality outputs for the same prompt. We define a tokenize_function that applies the tokenizer to each batch, encoding prompt-response pairs separately for accepted and rejected responses.

def tokenize_function(batch):
    tokenized_accepted = tokenizer(
        batch["prompt"], batch["accepted"], truncation=True, padding="max_length", max_length=128
    )
    tokenized_rejected = tokenizer(
        batch["prompt"], batch["rejected"], truncation=True, padding="max_length", max_length=128
    )
    return {
        "input_ids_accepted": tokenized_accepted["input_ids"],
        "attention_mask_accepted": tokenized_accepted["attention_mask"],
        "input_ids_rejected": tokenized_rejected["input_ids"],
        "attention_mask_rejected": tokenized_rejected["attention_mask"],
    }
tokenized_dataset = dataset.map(tokenize_function, batched=True)

The choice of tokenization settings can significantly impact model accuracy and training dynamics. For example,Truncation ensures that long inputs do not exceed the model’s maximum input length. However, truncating too aggressively may remove important context, especially in longer prompts or responses, leading to degraded reward predictions.Padding with "max_length" allows for consistent input sizes across batches, which is more efficient for training but may result in wasted computation for shorter sequences.Max length should be chosen carefully to balance efficiency and completeness. A limit that is too short may cut off essential information, while one that is too long can increase memory usage without clear performance gains.It's good practice to analyze the distribution of prompt and response lengths in your dataset to choose an appropriate max_length value. In some setups, dynamic padding (i.e., padding only up to the maximum length in a batch) may offer better performance.Once tokenized, the dataset is ready to be used as input for reward model training. Each example now contains two tokenized versions of the same prompt—one paired with a preferred (accepted) response and the other with a less preferred (rejected) response. These are used to train models that can assign higher scores to preferred completions. Whether training a neural reward model or implementing a simpler preference-ranking function, this tokenized structure provides a standardized, parallel format for computing relative quality signals, which are central to preference-based learning. In the next section we will explore techniques of reward modeling and bring in important concepts to consider during modeling.//

Exploring Techniques for Reward Modeling

Reward modeling for language modeling has mainly used supervised learning models trained on human preferences as data. Preference labels can be assigned to full behavioral trajectories—akin to inverse RL—or to individual input-output response pairs. Since crowd workers are humans providing a preference between a pair of output responses, the Bradley-Terry model of preference is suitable for measuring the probability of outcome where one response is preferred over another. The Bradley-Terry model is a probabilistic model that estimates the likelihood of one response being preferred over another. Given two responses r1​ and r2​, the model assumes the probability that r1 is preferred is given by:where s1​ and s2​ are the scalar scores assigned by the reward model to the respective responses. This formulation underlies the pairwise loss function used to train many reward models in RLHF.An alternative to pairwise comparisons is a regression-based approach, where the model is trained to predict scalar ratings (e.g., Likert-scale scores, a scale of five points ranging from strongly disagree to strongly agree). They also require crowd workers to use an ordinal scale for input. The HELPSTEER2-Preference paper: https://openreview.net/pdf?id=MnfHxPP5gs compares these two approaches and shows how complementing the two approaches can be beneficial.In practice, most reward models used in language fine-tuning rely on the pairwise preference format. Libraries like TRL support this approach directly through tools such as RewardTrainer.

Reward Modeling using TRL’s RewardTrainer and RewardConfig

The TRL library’s RewardTrainer module implements this pairwise preference–style reward modeling by optimizing the reward model to assign higher scores to preferred completions over rejected ones. It trains a RM from pairs of “chosen vs. rejected” samples, encouraging the model’s reward output to rank the chosen completion higher than the rejected one.RM can first initialize using similar method as for fine-tuning, using methods such as AutoModelForSequenceClassification from transformer library’s AutoModel methods. The RewardConfig method from trl library allows specifying training configuration for training the RM.

reward_model = AutoModelForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained(model_name, num_labels=1)
reward_config = RewardConfig(
    output_dir="./reward_model_output",  # Directory to save model
    max_length=128,  # Maximum sequence length
    per_device_train_batch_size=8,  # Training batch size
    per_device_eval_batch_size=8,  # Evaluation batch size
    learning_rate=5e-5,  # Learning rate
    num_train_epochs=3,  # Number of training epochs
    evaluation_strategy="steps",  # Evaluate periodically
    logging_strategy="steps",  # Logging configuration
    logging_steps=10,  # Log progress every 10 steps
    save_steps=500,  # Save model every 500 steps
    remove_unused_columns=False,  # Required for pretokenized datasets
    report_to="none",
)

In addition to specifying training configuration, similar to training configuration in case of fine-tuning, the RM training data is first processed into batches. This is facilitated by using Data Collator methods from transformer library. There are specific data collator methods such as DataCollatorWithPadding that create minibatches and pad data (or concatenate) where required, by using the tokenizer that needs to be passed. The data collators can then be passed to the model trainer RewardTrainer along with the training configurations RewardConfigs.

data_collator =DataCollatorWithPadding(tokenizer=tokenizer)
trainer = RewardTrainer( model=reward_model, args=reward_config, train_dataset=tokenized_dataset, eval_dataset=tokenized_dataset, data_collator=data_collator, # Use DataCollatorWithPadding tokenizer=tokenizer, # Pass tokenizer for preprocessing )

Here, we are using the tokenized dataset for demonstration purposes. In practice, the training dataset and eval dataset can be split, or carefully curated. Upon passing the reward configuration and data collator to trainer, the training and evaluation can be run similar to fine-tuning case we saw in chapter 6.

trainer.train()
results = trainer.evaluate()
print("Evaluation Results:", results)

The training output shows a table of chosen_text, rejected_text and logits. The logits are raw model predictions that are used to determine reward score. Logits can help calculate probabilities or make decisions about which output to accept or reject based on the RM’s criteria. The RM would evaluate candidate text by comparing its logits to those of the "ideal" text (the human-preferred output or a highly rated candidate). The responses are categorized into chosen text and rejected text based on model scoring and displayed in a training progress table, as shown in Table 7.1. //

The chosen_text and rejected_text columns have the representative dialog with the model e.g. question and generated answer. Since we are using a BERT type model, we see certain sentence specific tokens. CLS is the classification token, a special token added to the beginning of the input sequence. It is the aggregated representation of the input. During training, this token acts as a placeholder allowing aggregation of information across the entire input sequence, especially for tasks such as labeling and classifying. The SEP token (SEP stands for separation) is used to separate different segments and helps the model understand boundaries between different sentences or contexts within the input sequence. SEP also helps the model differentiate between the premise and the question, for instance here the first sentence is asking a question or for requesting some action, and second sentence is delivering or taking the action. Those tokens are exclusively because we are showing a BERT model output.Evaluation output shows key metrics that are typically reported after evaluating a model's performance during or after training. Evaluation outputs can be reported as follows:

Evaluation Results: {'eval_loss': 0.4644598960876465, 'eval_accuracy': 1.0, 'eval_runtime': 1.6874, 'eval_samples_per_second': 1.185, 'eval_steps_per_second': 0.593, 'epoch': 3.0}

The loss calculated during evaluation process is eval_loss, a measure of how well or poorly the model's predictions match the true values (ground truth). Similarly, eval_accuracy is the accuracy of the model on the evaluation dataset. Accuracy is the proportion of correctly predicted instances out of the total number of instances. eval_runtime is the total time taken to run the evaluation phase, measured in seconds. This can be useful for understanding the efficiency and speed of the evaluation process, especially when dealing with large datasets or when using complex models. Other evaluation metrics are eval_samples_per_second is a measure of model throughput showing us the number of evaluation samples processed per second during the evaluation. Similarly, eval_steps_per_second gives insights into the efficiency of model evaluation in terms of how quickly batches are processed, and it is influenced by factors like batch size, model complexity, and hardware. The epoch is specified during training, and is also presented with valuation, as it is a measure of how many times the model has seen the entire training dataset and can be useful for experimentation.In order to continue predicting for other data and testing with real-world scenarios, the predict method can be used, and tokenized_dataset can be replaced with other data, which is similarly structured. You can do a quick test using the same data, or use a different dataset (preferably a larger dataset), and check prediction for scores.

output = trainer.predict(tokenized_dataset)
predictions = output.predictions
print(predictions)
Adding Margin to Loss

There is a margin, which is the difference between these predicted scores for the "chosen" and "rejected" responses. It indicates how confident the model is in selecting one response over another. It is calculated using predicted scores above, and helps with decision-making. Assuming output.predictions contains the predicted scores for chosen and rejected responses, the margin can be calculated as their difference.

score_chosen = predictions[:, 0]
score_rejected = predictions[:, 1]
margin = score_chosen - score_rejected

You should compute the margin based on predicted scores because the margin represents the model's confidence in its choices and directly informs the training process by providing a signal for how well the model distinguishes between different responses. The evaluated scores are useful for tracking the model's performance but aren't used for calculating margins during training. According to HuggingFace TRL documentation, you can add margin to the loss by adding a margin column directly to the dataset. The reward collator will account for it by default, if the margin column exists. An example snippet showing how margin can be added to the dataset used in the above example is as follows:

def add_margin(row):
    return {'margin': row['score_chosen'] - row['score_rejected']}
dataset = dataset.map(add_margin)

dataset.map(add_margin) applies the add_margin function to every row in the dataset, resulting in a dataset with an added column (margin) containing the computed margin for each row.

Data Quality, Balance and Diversity

Data collection process involves several important considerations beyond user experience and scaling for user interaction and telemetry. The data quality is impacted by how the workers input the data, since human inputs involve subjectivity. The consistency in data quality can be improved by providing clear annotation guidelines, and checking for consistency. Similarly, the type of prompts provided to workers should have balanced coverage by including different types of responses by covering variety of topics and difficulty levels. If the model is given easy or happy-path type of questions, answers might lead to obvious correct answers, and workers rating them higher, giving an unbalanced score. Adding nuanced prompts and questions, or a variety, will help with more robust outcomes. Like coverage, diversity of annotators can help reduce systematic bias usually observed in a unipolar group.Before using the human annotated data for training a RM, it is important to assess the annotation quality. Measuring inter-annotator agreement and reliability helps with higher signal quality. Better agreement among annotators means higher confidence that annotated data correctly represents the correct signal. If human annotators differ widely on their preferences and judge outputs by a wide margin, it is indicative of requiring more data, as using such data can lead to suboptimal policies.There are several metrics to help with determining the quality of human annotators. One common metric that is widely used in computational linguistics, and is considered in reward modeling research is Cohen’s Kappa. It uses inter-annotator agreement and accounts for chance agreement. It usually has ranges from -1 to 1, where 1 denotes perfect agreement, 0 denotes agreement equivalent to chance, and -1 indicates systematic disagreement. If you have only two annotators and the labeling is strictly categorical (e.g., chosen vs. rejected), as in the case of pairwise preferences or likert-5 scale, Cohen’s kappa can be applicable and it gives a clear metric of how reliably the annotators agree. It can be represented mathematically as follows:Where is the observed agreement between raters or annotators, and is chance agreement between them. can be computed as the ratio of sum of chosen and rejected responses of samples that both annotators agree on and total samples. is the ratios of the sum of products of chosen responses of the two annotators and product of rejected responses of two annotators and total samples. Consider two raters, A and B, and their ratings as in Table 2:

A
Accept Reject
B Accept
Reject
N

Table 7.2: Inter-Rater Agreement TableHere, each of the table entries in Table 7.2 denote accepted and rejected responses by rater A or rater B. Suffices are added to distinguish whether both raters agreed ‘A_B’ or a single rater agreed ‘A’ only. N with suffices denotes those specific scenarios and T denotes total accepted or rejected responses by A (for total scenarios where A accepted) or B. N without suffices, is total number of accepted and rejected scenarios. Considering this table, the observed agreement and chance agreement can be represented by mathematical equations as follows:Cohen’s kappa helps determine whether data is balanced or unbalanced, and is higher for balanced data. However, it must be used in conjunction with accuracy metric, as it does not say much about accuracy. This metric can be further extended to scale to multiple raters, and the extended version is called Fleish Kappa.Quadratic Weighted Kappa is a variant of Cohen’s kappa designed for ordinal data—ratings that follow a natural order (e.g., 1 < 2 < 3 < 4 < 5). It applies a weighting scheme that penalizes disagreements more as the distance between the assigned ratings grows. For instance, disagreeing on “1” vs. “5” is a bigger error than disagreeing on “3” vs. “4.”Fleiss’ kappa is a statistical measure of inter-rater reliability that generalizes Cohen’s kappa to more than two raters. The formulation for Fleiss’s kappa is similar to that of Cohen’s kappa, but calculation of observed agreement and chance agreement are modified to account for multiple raters, is mean of the agreement probability over all raters, and is mean agreement probability over all raters if they were randomly assigned.Krippendorff’s alpha is a more general measure of reliability that can handle different data types (nominal, ordinal, interval, ratio) and different numbers of annotators. It also accounts for missing data, which often arises in large-scale annotation projects.Artstein and Poesio’s “Inter-Coder Agreement for Computational Linguistics” (2008) is a comprehensive survey on measuring and interpreting inter-coder agreement in computational linguistics. They discuss:

  • Different agreement measures (including kappa statistics and alpha coefficients).
  • When to use which measure depending on the nature of the data (nominal vs. ordinal vs. interval).
  • Pitfalls and best practices: how to design annotation tasks to reduce confusion, how to handle chance correction, and how reliability relates to validity.

If you are interested in more depth, you can refer to the online pdf here: https://direct.mit.edu/coli/article/34/4/555/1999/Inter-Coder-Agreement-for-Computational or here: https://aclanthology.org/J08-4004.pdf .Ensuring that annotators understand the definitions and instructions—and that they consistently apply them—is crucial. Artstein and Poesio’s guidelines: https://aclanthology.org/J08-4004.pdf can help pinpoint which reliability measure is most appropriate for your RLHF setting and how to interpret it.Note that these metrics can be used to evaluate data quality prior to using it for training. Often preprocessing the data based on these metrics will help improve chances of training a RM that is aligned with representative population of annotators. In addition to rating individual prompt responses, the quality of responses can be dependent on context input to the model. Response is in-distribution to trained model, if model is trained for responses as part of conversation, with input as conversational history. If input is similar to what the model has seen during training, response is more likely to be as expected. Considerations around quality of input are important, and we will look at them next.

Other considerations for Reward Modeling

Reward modeling is supervised learning from data, where preprocessed data has prompt input and responses from model as inputs, and preference ratings from annotators as reward score output. While quality of data is an important factor in how well the RM aligns with human preferences, there are some other considerations for training a well aligned model.

Context and Chat History in Reward Modeling

The response of a model when a single input is provided can be different compared to the response as part of a multi-turn conversation. The additional information based on conversational history can help the model respond in alignment to the topic of conversation. The process of assessing data quality, especially the metrics will not change much though. The annotators can rate response for the entire conversation or for the entire set of responses for that conversation session. It can also be rating each response and using cumulative score.

Dealing with Underspecification and Overoptimization

RMs are inherently underspecified. They perform well when data is in-distribution of the training data, but the outputs can be unexpected when there is a distribution shift. Underspecification of RMs occurs because RMs are based on training using human feedback signals, and human feedback is only a proxy signal of thorough human evaluation. This results in overoptimization, where the model is aligned with human feedback that is not specifying the desired outcome accurately. Using an ensemble of the signals from multiple RMs can mitigate overoptimization as shown in https://arxiv.org/pdf/2312.09244. There are also other approaches of mitigating overoptimization, as in https://openreview.net/pdf?id=gkfUvn0fLU, it tries to show that constraining reinforcement learning can help with mitigating overoptimization.

Model Evaluation and Iteration

In the last section, we showed how RewardTrainer can be used for training a RM. After training, we also showed how using the eval method the trained model can be evaluated. For demonstration, we used the same tokenized data. But, in case of using real dataset, you have to split the dataset into a large training data and a small subset excluded from training data as evaluation data. This is the first level of evaluation. Additional evaluations post-training, can be incorporated from here on.

Evaluation Steps and Metrics

Some evaluation approaches can be extension of the evaluation passed to RewardTrainer. These can be in the form of held-out human comparison with following steps:

  1. Collect a test data set similar to training set or use part of training data excluded from training,
  2. Have human raters rate or rank or add preferences to each of the input and the pair of responses presented (in case of pairwise preference scoring).
  3. Then compare the RM’s predicted score with human rating.

In order to compare how well the RM predicted with respect to human rating, multiple scores can be used simultaneously.

Accuracy or Agreement Rate

Take the two responses from test data, e.g. response1 and response2. The RM will predict scores for each of these responses, e.g. R(response1) and R(response2). Agreement rate or accuracy, here, computes how many times RM’s prediction matches human preference data. Agreement rate =

Rank Correlation Metrics

When the dataset has multiple responses compared with ranking based scale, this metric is more appropriate as it measure degree to which the two rankings agree. It measures how well overall rank ordering predicted by RM aligns with what humans might have ranked. For example, let us consider the case where instead of pairwise ranking, humans score multiple responses in order of preference e.g. response3 is best, followed by response 5, then response 2. The rank correlation computation involves following steps: First, the responses are ranked by the RM e.g. R(response1), R(response2),… This is followed by sorting the scores by their score values, to get the RM’s ranking. Then, the RM’s ranking is compared to human ranking.The comparison of RM with human ranking, can use Spearman’s , which uses the ranked positions and computes the standard correlation coefficient : , where cov is for covariance between RM score and human preference, and the metric is ranging between -1 and 1. Similarly, another metric comparing rank ordering, Kendall’s can also be computed. It measures the strength of association, and in simplified form can be represented as :.

RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error) and MAE (Mean Absolute Error)

Depending upon the subject domain and response types, some applications might require scores to be scalar values ranging between 0-100 or 0-1, or even a likert-scale 1-5. In such cases, standard regression metrics such as Root Mean Squared Error (RSME) or mean absolute error (MAE) can be used. Thes scores provide standard quality scores, and are good for determining magnitude of closeness.While these metrics are good for ensuring model training best practices are followed, before using in real-world applications, additional generalization tests should be conducted. Some of these tests can be as follows.

Out-of-distribution Robustness

Out-of-distribution robustness is mainly a test of model generalization, to test whether the model can perform well on data that has not been part of distribution of the training data. This requires collecting data, which is dissimilar to training data, and testing how model scores compare to human preferences. If model scores disagree with human preferences, all the systematic differences can be observed and, in those examples, feature engineering-based inputs should be collected for either retraining or fine-tuning of RM. While this step can be exhaustive, it is a necessary step for real world applications. The best practice is to use the model in real-world and test for out-of-distribution robustness, collect the data, retrain or fine-tune on it, and then re-test. This can be a periodic repetitive process.

Policy Performance

No matter how well the RM performs, its eventual purpose is to help train policy that will align with human intent for the language model. Therefore, the policy performance can be a practical metric for testing whether RM is useful. It is similar to the integration tests performed for software modules. Good policy performance however, does not guarantee that the RM is robust. It is only an indirect indication that given RM is directionally correct. The converse, a good RM does not guarantee better performance either, and often moderate accuracy is enough, as per recent findings in this paper, The Accuracy Paradox in RLHF: When Better Reward Models Don’t Yield Better Language Models: https://aclanthology.org/2024.emnlp-main.174.pdf. The RM might not be a perfect reflection of human preferences in all scenarios, but generally it is providing enough signal to push the policy toward behaviors that humans generally like. It is totally possible that it has captured partial correlations and is useful for the tests or examples used for testing policy performance. Therefore, in practice it is not sufficient.

Indicators of Poor RM-Induced Policy Behavior

Apart from the evaluation metrics we discussed in the last section, there are some indicators of policy behavior resulting from poor reward model accuracy. Some of them are as follows:

  • Preference Misalignment: A fine-tuned policy may generate outputs that humans do not actually prefer, despite the reward model showing high accuracy on its validation set. This suggests that the RM overfits superficial patterns in the preference data (e.g., length, sentiment) rather than capturing deeper intent.
  • Reward Hacking: Policies often learn to game the reward model. Common symptoms include overly verbose completions, excessive hedging by adding "As an AI language model..." to start a sentence, or moralizing statements designed to trigger high reward scores. This issue frequently arises from deficiencies in the reward model, which stem from human labelers typically assigning higher ratings to longer texts.
  • Behavior Collapse or Mode Narrowing: Poor reward shaping may cause the policy to generate overly similar or conservative outputs, hurting diversity, informativeness, or creativity. This is especially problematic in open-ended tasks like summarization or dialogue.
Strategies to Address Poor Policy Performance

While poor RM performance can be fixed using supervised learning strategies similar to traditional ML approaches such as data balancing, regularization, and out-of-distribution effect (discussed in previous section), the metrics do not capture some their performance issues affecting policy behavior. When poor policy performance reveals RM issues, several strategies can help:

  • Refine and expand preference data: Target areas where the policy underperforms. Collect more comparisons involving current policy outputs, especially failure cases.
  • Apply active relabeling: Sample uncertain, low-confidence, or controversial completions for fresh labeling. This helps improve the reward model where it lacks clear guidance.
  • Clip or normalize rewards: Prevent extreme reward values from destabilizing policy updates. Helps mitigate reward hacking.
  • Use ensemble reward models: Reduces overfitting to specific annotator quirks or biases, and improves generalization across prompt types.
  • Evaluate with policy probing checkpoints: Use intermediate policies to test reward model robustness over the course of fine-tuning.

In practice, the ultimate test for RM is how the final policy trained—fine-tuned language model based on RM—behaves. Besides, RM accuracy and impact of policy behavior, the policy training also contributes to how the model responses are, we will cover these in the next chapter.

Summary

In this chapter, we explored the end-to-end process of building a reward model for language modeling. We covered preference data collection, preprocessing best practices, and training techniques using the TRL and Transformers libraries. To ensure data reliability, we discussed the use of inter-rater agreement metrics such as Cohen’s Kappa and Krippendorff’s Alpha. We emphasized the importance of margin-based loss for capturing preference confidence, and highlighted key factors like data quality, balance, diversity, and the role of chat context. Challenges such as underspecification and reward overoptimization were also addressed. Finally, we outlined evaluation strategies and reaffirmed the reward model’s role in guiding reinforcement learning for fine-tuning the base language model—covered next in Chapter 8.

Chapter 8: Reinforcement Learning For Tuning Language Models

Join our book community on Discord

The goal of this chapter is to provide both a practical and conceptual understanding of how reinforcement learning is used to fine-tune a pre-trained language model using a reward model trained on human preference data. This phase completes the reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) pipeline by transforming preference signals into an optimization target, enabling measurable and aligned model behavior using stable reinforcement learning techniques such as Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO).You will learn how to integrate a trained reward model into a reinforcement learning loop, fine-tune a policy model using that reward signal, and implement this pipeline using tools such as the Transformer Reinforcement Learning (TRL) library. The chapter builds directly on supervised fine-tuning (Chapter 6) and reward modeling (Chapter 7), with a focus on closing the loop and aligning generation behavior with human expectations.This final phase of RLHF is critical because it turns implicit human feedback into explicit optimization. Without it, models remain only partially aligned; with it, they become responsive to the values, goals, and safety constraints that matter in real-world deployment. Mastering this step is essential for building language models that are not only capable, but also trustworthy and aligned.Key topics include:

  • Integration of Reward Models with Reinforcement Learning
  • PPO algorithm for RLHF
  • Fine tuning using PPO Trainer in Transformer Reinforcement Learning(TRL) Library
  • Metrics to Track in PPO Fine-Tuning
  • Evaluating the fine-tuned model

Technical requirements

  2. numpy == 1.26.4
  3. matplotlib == 3.8.4
