Integration of reward models with reinforcement learning

Reward models (discussed in Chapter 7 in details) are useful in simulating how humans score or give preferences, when presented with example choices. Trained reward models can be used for improving policy optimization and aligning the language models to human preferences. A high-level schematic of how reward models are connected with other components of RLHF is shown in Figure 8.1. A reward model outputs the signal (reward) or score on generated model response for given user prompts. During training it uses human annotated scores or preferences to compare its prediction, for improving the model. Once trained, the model outputs reward or scores for input texts. This reward is used to calculate how a policy model can be updated.

In practical RLHF setting, generally the state-action pairs provided as input to the reward model can be user prompts and their model generated responses. The user prompt can be questions, chat messages , images or any other form of data, and the model may be asked to generate a text response, which can be a chat message or reply, an image or code, or simply question-answer pairs. The output from reward model (reward) is used for updating policy model weights, and the update process is based on reinforcement learning. The reward model is already trained on large relevant samples of user prompts and example responses with human scores.Unlike standard RL where a reward function calculates the reward based on observations or reward assignment for observations are explicitly mapped, RLHF relies on predictions of reward model. This model is generally based on a neural network or a preference model such as the Bradley Terry model. This means instead of explicit reward assignment, the predicted reward is an estimate of the desirability or quality of state-action pairs based on human feedback. In standard RL, the computed reward determines how much the weights of policy model need to be updated. In this case, we use reward model inference instead, to update policy model. For applications such as those involving natural language, explicit reward assignment is not an option, and reward models are a practical alternative.Reward models are based on training using human annotated state action pairs, which can be inconsistent, subjective, noisy and depend upon the context. Generalization of reward inference to novel scenarios is a requirement, and this often expected in practice. Fortunately, the uncertainty in learned rewards and generalizability can be dealt with in a similar way as we would handle any supervised learning model.Predicted rewards from a RM are usually normalized to make sure they are within an operating range. Usually this is based on experimentation, so that instability in policy optimization can be minimized. The output from reward models, the reward signals, can be used for training the policy model. Policy optimization can be experimented with any of the RL algorithms, however, on-policy algorithms, especially PPO are preferred due to their relatively stable training. In the next section we will discuss more on PPO algorithm.

PPO algorithm for RLHF

We covered PPO algorithm in Chapter 4, in a lot of depth. In this section, we will discuss it from perspective of RLHF’s standard algorithm choice because PPO provides a good balances with respect to training stability and sample efficiency. Among on-policy algorithms, it has simpler implementation of clipping mechanism that attenuates policy updates from previous iteration, and thereby improves stability. While off-policy algorithms can be sample efficient, PPO improves sample efficiency by reusing collected data for multiple updates making it more sample efficient compared to other policy gradient algorithms. Because of its balance between stability and efficiency, and its tested efficacy across a variety of environments, it is a default choice across RL applications including RLHF. It’s wide adoption is also due to its relative stability and reliability in training, which is necessary for managing training of large systems with uncertainty.

PPO policy update using reward models

In standard PPO, the policy is trained using a reward signal computed by a reward function or an assigned based on the observations from the environment. However, in RLHF, the reward function is learned from human preferences, which introduces unique challenges and requires some necessary modifications.In lieu of a static reward from environment, a learned reward function, , where represents the parameters of the reward model helps update the PPO policy. The optimization objective is more or less same as the standard PPO but now incorporates the learned reward based on reward model :The general key policy update steps for PPO will therefore be:

  1. Collecting Rollouts: Gathering trajectories for policy .
  2. Infer the reward model on collected state-action pairs to obtain predicted rewards.
  3. Compute advantage estimates , which indicates how much better the action taken was compared to the average action at time and sample t, using Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE).
  4. Compute the PPO objective (see Chapter 4 for complete implementation) using:
  5. Update the gradient using stochastic gradient ascent.

Modifications needed to adapt PPO for RLHF

While PPO is the default algorithm to consider when working with RLHF policy optimization, there are some challenges to it:

  • Addressing Distribution Shift: Since the reward model was trained on past policy rollouts or human-labeled data, it may encounter distributional shifts as the policy evolves. This necessitates periodic re-training or fine-tuning of the reward model using up-to-date trajectories sampled from the latest policy to maintain alignment with human intent. Techniques such as reward model ensembling or uncertainty estimation can also mitigate overfitting to outdated preferences.
  • Regularization and Safety Constraints: To prevent unintended policy behaviors, constraints can be incorporated during optimization. Some of these are already considered as part of PPO implementation. For example:
    • Entropy Regularization which encourages exploration to prevent premature convergence,
    • KL Divergence Constraints ensures policy updates do not diverge too far from prior policies to maintain stability.
    • Reward Model uncertainty handling by penalizing high-uncertainty reward predictions to avoid over-reliance on unreliable signals can also help.
  • Evaluation and Iterative Refinement: Evaluation of the policy trained with inferred rewards is an important step. These evaluations involve monitoring quantitative metrics such as reward accumulation and success rate. Similarly, qualitative assessments in the form of human evaluations and preference comparisons can be incorporated. Based on these evaluations, adjustments to the reward model or fine-tuning steps can enhance alignment and stability.

By integrating inferred rewards into policy optimization, RLHF enables policies to better align with human preferences, facilitating more interpretable and effective reinforcement learning outcomes.

RLHF for Natural Language Applications

PPO algorithm steps discussed in the previous section require collecting roll-out trajectories. In natural language applications such as chat and dialog generation, the concept of a trajectory is adapted to suit natural language interactions. In this context, a roll-out refers to a generated sequence—typically a model response—produced in response to a prompt or user message. Instead of the typical sequence of states and actions seen in classical reinforcement learning, the "trajectory" corresponds to a structured conversation or a prompt-response pair, where each token can be viewed as an action taken by the policy. There are two possible ways to frame the term “trajectory” based on model interaction scenario.Full Conversation as a Trajectory: In some implementations, an entire conversation thread (e.g., multiple turns of user queries and model responses) is treated as a trajectory. The model generates multiple responses sequentially, forming a trajectory that is then evaluated with a reward model or human feedback.Single question-answer (QA) Pair as a Trajectory: In a more simplified version, each individual question-response pair is treated as a separate "episode," and a reward is assigned based on its quality.The choice between these depends on the granularity of the feedback and how the reward model is trained. Next, we will consider how reward and feedback is considered for these trajectories.

Reward and Feedback

The data structure of reward and feedback from human preferences is determined during reward model training. In a multi-turn conversation, the reward can be collected per-turn if each response is evaluated separately. Some approaches involve assigning a single reward to the entire conversation, and this can consider metrics such as coherence, helpfulness and consistency, across turns. More commonly, a preference based reward is used, where a reward model is trained based on human preferences (e.g., ranking multiple responses). This model then assigns scores to responses during RL training.

Training Process

The training process involves specific steps. First, the language model generates responses either as full conversations or question – answer (QA) pairs. These responses are assigned rewards based on the learned reward model incorporating human preferences. Then, the PPO algorithm optimizes the foundational model using this reward using these steps:

  1. Collecting trajectories (either conversations or QA pairs): The model generates outputs by sampling from its current policy. Each output is treated as a trajectory, corresponding to either a full multi-turn interaction or a single QA pair, depending on the framing used. These samples serve as the basis for reward evaluation and learning.
  2. Estimating advantage functions using a baseline: Each trajectory is scored by the reward model, which reflects human preference data. To determine how much the model should reinforce or discourage a given output, we compute the advantage—the difference between the actual reward and a baseline (often a value function). This helps focus learning on better-than-expected outputs while mitigating variance in policy gradients.
  3. Perform policy updates with a KL constraint: The policy is updated using the PPO objective to increase the probability of high-reward outputs. Crucially, PPO includes a KL divergence penalty that limits how much the updated policy can diverge from the reference policy (usually the supervised fine-tuned model). This constraint preserves the fluency and coherence learned during earlier fine-tuning stages while encouraging alignment with human preferences.
Challenges in PPO for Chat Applications

The reward model consistency is a challenge as conversational quality is subjective and so are collected human preferences. The collection process should therefore be conducted using the best practices, as we discussed in Chapter 7, and these are still evolving due to nascency of the technology. Chat conversations can get long, and longer conversations introduce instability in training, making per-turn rewards more practical in some cases. Unlike usual RL environments such as games and simulations, exploring diverse responses from human conversational data and maintaining quality can be challenging. Therefore, generally, in practice chat models typically use question-response pairs with preference-based ranking, rather than full multi-turn conversations, to stabilize training and make reward modeling more tractable.

Fine-tuning using PPOTrainer in TRL

As of 2025, several open-source libraries have emerged for RLHF, enabling developers and researchers to fine-tune large language models more effectively. Among the major open-source libraries for RLHF, the prominent libraries are Transformer Reinforcement Learning (TRL) library, an advanced distributed version forked from it namely TRLx designed to accommodate distributed fine tuning of large models with billions of parameters. Reinforcement Learning for Language Models (RL4LMs) is a versatile library that provides implementations of multiple on-policy RL algorithms, with a novel optimization natural language policy optimization(NLPO), based on PPO. Since TRL is the most widely adopted library, and often a basis for others such as TRLx, we will focus on that implementation. TRL is also a good initiation into fine-tuning.In the previous chapter, we used RewardTrainer class for training a reward model. In this section, we demonstrate how to incorporate a trained reward model—or a suitable pretrained alternative—into the RLHF pipeline. For the sake of illustration, we use a pretrained reward model discussed in the Reward Model Setup section. To fine-tune the policy model (the language model being optimized) using this reward signal, we use the PPOTrainer class .Before we begin fine-tuning a language model using PPOTrainer, it’s important to set up a suitable environment for RLHF training. The following steps will guide you through preparing a GPU-enabled setup using Colab Pro, with notes on how to adapt this to other environments such as AWS, Azure, or a local machine.

  • Adjust batch size and sequence length to fit within available memory.

Settings and Configurations for Memory Optimization and Reproducibility

For running notebook experiments, it is crucial to be able to reproduce experiments, and improve iteratively. One of the best practices is to keep notebook results relatively reproducible is to set a seed for randomization. A snippet of seed settings to enable reproducibility is as follows:

SEED = 42
torch.manual_seed(SEED)
np.random.seed(SEED)
set_seed(SEED)

Here, we are using a lightweight model,gpt2, in order reduce memory usage. In addition to this choice, there are a few more parametric trimming that we are using here. For the example we consider, we will limit generated text length by keeping MAX_NEW_TOKENS = 32, a small batch size for memory efficiency BATCH_SIZE = 2 or 4, truncated sequence length MAX_SEQ_LENGTH = 32, and a small number of training samples NUM_SAMPLES = 4000, and NUM_EPOCHS = 2. To ensure stable training and efficient memory usage, we use optimizations such as gradient checkpointing and mixed precision (set to true for larger models).

# Configuration - Memory-optimized settings
MODEL_NAME = "gpt2" 
MAX_NEW_TOKENS = 32 
BATCH_SIZE = 4 
MAX_SEQ_LENGTH = 32 
LR = 1.5e-5
NUM_EPOCHS = 2 
NUM_SAMPLES = 4000 
OPTIMIZE_MEMORY = True
if OPTIMIZE_MEMORY:
    # Enable gradient checkpointing (trades computation for memory)
    os.environ["PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF"] = "expandable_segments:True"
    MIXED_PRECISION = False

To optimize memory usage during training, particularly when working with large models or batch processing, it is useful to define a utility function to clear unused memory resources. The function clear_memory() invokes Python’s garbage collector and clears the CUDA cache, while also resetting peak memory statistics. It is important to exercise caution and call this function only between training batches or iterations to avoid disrupting active computations. Incorporating clear_memory() at appropriate points within the training loop can help mitigate out-of-memory errors and maintain efficient GPU memory utilization:

def clear_memory():
    import gc
    gc.collect()
    if torch.cuda.is_available():
        torch.cuda.empty_cache()
        torch.cuda.reset_peak_memory_stats()

After setting configuration for memory optimization and requirements, we look at data preparation as the next step.

Data Preparation

For demonstration purposes, we will use a small dataset available on hugging face, but you can apply the same method for real world datasets. We can load a small subset of the Anthropic Helpful-Harmless (HH-RLHF) dataset, a method from datasets HuggingFace library, load_dataset("Anthropic/hh-rlhf"). To limit the size of data, you can split the training data using NUM_SAMPLES.

dataset = load_dataset("Anthropic/hh-rlhf", split=f"train[:{NUM_SAMPLES}]")

It contains human-written prompts along with assistant responses labeled as "chosen" (preferred) and "rejected" (less preferred). For efficient fine-tuning, we preprocess the dataset by extracting prompts and their respective chosen and rejected responses. In addition, we filter out overly long prompts for memory efficiency and handle potential formatting inconsistencies. Prompts and responses are tokenized and formatted for use in the RLHF training loop. An example function that preprocesses the dataset is shown as follows:

def prepare_dataset(dataset):
    data_dict = {'prompt': [], 'chosen': [], 'rejected': []}
    for item in dataset:
        try:
            prompt = item['chosen'].split('Human:')[-1].split('Assistant:')[0].strip()
            chosen = item['chosen'].split('Assistant:')[-1].strip()
            rejected = item['rejected'].split('Assistant:')[-1].strip()
            if len(prompt.split()) < 100:
                data_dict['prompt'].append(prompt)
                data_dict['chosen'].append(chosen)
                data_dict['rejected'].append(rejected)
        except:
            continue
    return pd.DataFrame(data_dict)prepare dataset

To efficiently fine-tune RLHF models, we define a custom dataset class that processes the Helpful-Harmless dataset. The preprocessed dataset consists of prompts, along with assistant responses labeled as "chosen" and "rejected." To optimize memory usage, the class pre-tokenize the prompts and store them as memory-efficient tensors. The tokenized data is then fed directly into the PPOTrainer, where it is used for fine-tuning the policy network. This avoids redundant tokenization calls during training. The dataset class also ensures that input sequences are properly truncated to a fixed length for consistent model training. While you can check full implementation of the class with comments in the notebook expanding into methods for getting additional attributes, an example trimmed version is shown as follows.

class RLHFDataset(Dataset):
    def __init__(self, data, tokenizer, max_length=MAX_SEQ_LENGTH):
        self.tokenizer = tokenizer
        self.prompts = data['prompt'].tolist()
        self.max_length = max_length
        self.input_ids = []
        self.attention_masks = []
        self.preprocess()
    def preprocess(self):
        for prompt in self.prompts:
            encodings = self.tokenizer(
                prompt,
                truncation=True,
                max_length=self.max_length,
                padding="max_length",
                return_tensors="pt"
        )     
        self.input_ids.append(encodings['input_ids'].squeeze())
        self.attention_masks.append(encodings['attention_mask'].squeeze())

Reward Model Setup

The RewardTrainer method from the TRL library implements a pairwise preference–style reward modeling approach. It trains a reward model using “chosen vs. rejected” response pairs — as we explored in depth in Chapter 7.In this chapter, instead of training from scratch, we demonstrate how to use a pretrained reward model. A strong option is OpenAssistant/reward-model-deberta-v3-base, which is trained on human preference data and suitable for real RLHF fine-tuning:

reward_tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("OpenAssistant/reward-model-deberta-v3-base")
reward_model = AutoModelForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained(
    "OpenAssistant/reward-model-deberta-v3-base"
).to(DEVICE)

In early prototyping stages, you may consider using proxy reward signals from models like sentiment classifiers or toxicity detectors, which offer fast inference and easy integration. For example:distilbert-base-uncased-finetuned-sst-2-english provides a score based on sentiment (positive or negative).unitary/toxic-bert outputs toxicity likelihood to help penalize unsafe or harmful language.These models are standard classifiers and are not trained on human preference data, so their scores may not fully capture nuanced behaviors such as helpfulness, factual accuracy, or overall alignment. Nonetheless, they can be useful for quick experimentation or task-specific objectives like reducing toxicity.For this chapter, we use OpenAssistant/reward-model-deberta-v3-base, a purpose-built reward model trained on preference data, to demonstrate a complete and realistic RLHF training pipeline grounded in human-aligned reward signals.In RLHF, the policy model generates responses that are evaluated by a reward model, which assigns a scalar reward score reflecting human preference alignment. To efficiently compute rewards over multiple responses, we process the texts in small batches, tokenize them, and run inference through the reward model. The resulting scores are collected and returned as a list of reward tensors, which are then used to guide policy optimization in the PPOTrainer. An example implementation of the reward computation function, compute_reward, is shown below.

def compute_reward(texts):
    batch_size = 4
    all_scores = []
    for i in range(0, len(texts), batch_size):
        batch_texts = texts[i:i + batch_size]
        inputs = reward_tokenizer(
            batch_texts,
            return_tensors="pt",
            padding=True,
            truncation=True,
            max_length=512,
        ).to(DEVICE)
        with torch.no_grad():
            outputs = reward_model(**inputs)
            scores = outputs.logits.squeeze(-1)
            all_scores.extend(scores.cpu().tolist())
    return [torch.tensor(score) for score in all_scores]

Using LoRA for Efficient Fine-Tuning

Fine-tuning large language models can be memory-intensive. To address this, we use LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation)—a PEFT method discussed in Chapter 6. LoRA introduces trainable low-rank matrices into attention and feedforward layers, drastically reducing the number of trainable parameters while keeping the base model frozen.We define a LoRA configuration and pass it into the model via the peft_config parameter:

from peft import LoraConfig, TaskType
lora_config = LoraConfig(
    r=8,
    lora_alpha=16,
    target_modules=["c_attn", "c_proj", "c_fc"],
    lora_dropout=0.05,
    bias="none",
    task_type=TaskType.CAUSAL_LM
)

Model Initialization

To efficiently manage memory while fine-tuning a language model using RLHF, we carefully sequence the loading of models, tokenizer, and dataset. Before initializing any components, we invoke the utility function clear_memory() to release unused memory resources and prevent unnecessary peak allocation.The primary language model we fine-tune is gpt2 (you may also use distilgpt2 a lighter distilled version) which we load using AutoModelForCausalLMWithValueHead. This wrapper from the trl library allows integration of a scalar value head used in PPO-based RLHF training. We enable gradient checkpointing to reduce memory consumption during backpropagation, trading off some additional computation.The reference model (ref_model) is then loaded separately to avoid GPU memory spikes. It is a frozen copy of the policy model and serves as a comparison baseline during reward-based optimization. It is used during PPO training to compute KL divergence between the current and original model outputs.

clear_memory()
model = AutoModelForCausalLMWithValueHead.from_pretrained(
    MODEL_NAME,  # e.g., "gpt2"
    peft_config=lora_config,
    use_cache=not OPTIMIZE_MEMORY
)
# Optionally enable gradient checkpointing
# model.gradient_checkpointing_enable()
# torch.compile(model)  # Optional with PyTorch 2.x
clear_memory()
ref_model = AutoModelForCausalLMWithValueHead.from_pretrained(
    MODEL_NAME,
    peft_config=lora_config,
    use_cache=not OPTIMIZE_MEMORY
)

After the models are initialized, we load the tokenizer and dataset. The dataset is processed through the RLHFDataset class, which structures the data for use in training. This staged initialization ensures memory is allocated incrementally, improving stability and scalability.

tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL_NAME)
tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token  # Ensure compatibility with padding
rlhf_dataset = RLHFDataset(prepared_data, tokenizer)

While GPT-2 is not extremely large compared to recent models, it still occupies significant GPU memory. Loading it before the dataset ensures that the most memory-intensive component is allocated first, avoiding out-of-memory errors. Additionally, tokenizers are typically tied to the model architecture and must be initialized using the same model name. Creating the dataset afterward ensures that memory is used incrementally and predictably during setup, which is essential for stable RLHF training on limited hardware.Now, that we have model initialized and dataset prepared and loaded, we can start discussing the RLHF training loop.

RLHF Training Loop

The training or fine-tuning of the language model involves integrating the data, reward model, and both the base and reference model together. This is necessary for training the base model. This is a two step process: 1) structuring data and instantiating PPOTrainer, 2) setting up the training loop. We look at both in depth next.

Structuring data and PPOTrainer instantiation

Before model training begins, the dataset must be structured efficiently to align with the needs of PPO-based optimization. This is where the data collator plays a central role. A data collator is responsible for gathering multiple samples into a batch while ensuring that all inputs are properly formatted.Unlike supervised learning, where batches consist of fixed-length token sequences, RLHF requires flexible data structures, including:

  • Raw prompts from the dataset.
  • Chosen and rejected responses, used to compute rewards and policy updates.
  • Tokenized sequences, which must be padded and formatted consistently.

The data collator collects all relevant fields into a structured batch, ensuring that each training step processes multiple examples efficiently without unnecessary memory overhead. The following snippet is a sample example of how it can be implemented.

def collator(data):
    return {key: [d[key] for d in data] for key in data[0].keys()}

Before instantiating the PPOTrainer, we must define a PPOConfig—a configuration object that governs the key hyperparameters for the training process. This includes settings like learning rate, batch size, KL-divergence penalty type, and memory optimization flags. Defining this configuration object is essential because the PPOTrainer uses it internally to schedule learning updates, control divergence from the reference model, and optimize memory usage during training.Here is an example PPO configuration designed for training under resource constraints:

# PPO Configuration with memory-optimized settings
config = PPOConfig(
    model_name=MODEL_NAME,
    learning_rate=LR,
    batch_size=BATCH_SIZE,
    mini_batch_size=2,
    reward_model="OpenAssistant/reward-model-deberta-v3-base",
    query_dataset="Anthropic/hh-rlhf",
    kl_penalty="kl",
    optimize_cuda_cache=True,
    log_with=None
)

Once the configuration is defined, we can instantiate the PPOTrainer. The PPOTrainer, implemented in the trl library, orchestrates the fine-tuning process. It takes in several components:

  • A policy model (the model being fine-tuned), model.
  • A reference model (a frozen copy of the original model, used to prevent over-optimization), ref_model.
  • A reward model (which in reality, scores generated responses based on human feedback), reward_model.
  • A dataset (containing prompts, preferred responses, and rejected responses), dataset.
  • The tokenizer and data collator, collator,

An example PPOTrainer instantiation is given here:

ppo_trainer = PPOTrainer(
    config=config,
    model=model,
    ref_model=ref_model,
    tokenizer=tokenizer,
    dataset=rlhf_dataset,
    data_collator=collator
)

This order of construction—starting with configuration and followed by trainer instantiation—is essential to maintain modularity, reproducibility, and code clarity. It also ensures that training-specific optimizations like memory handling and batch scheduling are consistently applied during PPO updates.

Tip: Understanding Advanced PPOConfig Parameters

PPOConfig offers several tunable fields to control training dynamics, including:

init_kl_coef, target_kl, and adap_kl_ctrl for adaptive KL regularization.

cliprange and cliprange_value to prevent unstable updates.

optimize_cuda_cache to reduce memory fragmentation on GPU.

log_with for flexible integration with tracking tools like wandb or tensorboard.

These allow you to balance reward maximization, stability, and memory efficiency—critical for RLHF fine-tuning on resource-limited hardware.

The PPOTrainer method sets the PPO training, as per the intended goal of PPO algorithm, it balances reward maximization and stability by constraining updates to prevent drastic changes, ensuring steady improvements in model behavior.During RLHF fine-tuning, generation parameters govern how the language model produces outputs and must be specified before entering the training loop. Proper tuning of these parameters ensures that the model remains fluent and coherent while balancing creativity and adherence to learned behavior. These are passed as generation_kwargs, during the training loop. Key parameters include:

  • top_p (Nucleus Sampling): Restricts the sampling pool to the most probable tokens, controlling diversity. Higher values (e.g., 0.9) allow for more varied outputs. Lower values (e.g., 0.3) make responses more deterministic.
  • do_sample (Sampling Activation): Enables randomness in token selection instead of always picking the most probable token.
  • max_new_tokens (Response Length Control): Limits the number of tokens generated to prevent overly long or short responses.

A sample snippet is as follows:

generation_kwargs = {
    "top_p": 0.9, 
    "do_sample": True, 
    "max_new_tokens": 32
}

These settings directly influence response diversity, coherence, and quality and will be passed to the ppo_trainer during the training loop for generation of response.

Setting Up Training Loop

Once the PPO training parameters are set up and data is structured for batch training, the RLHF fine-tuning process follows an iterative training loop where the model generates responses, receives feedback in the form of rewards, and updates its policy using PPO. Each training step consists of the following key stages:1. Generating Model Responses: The model takes a prompt as input and generates a response using predefined sampling parameters (generation_kwargs).2. Computing Rewards: The generated response is evaluated by a reward model that scores how well the output aligns with human feedback.3. Updating the Policy: The PPOTrainer instance uses the computed rewards to optimize the model's policy, improving future responses. A KL-divergence constraint helps maintain stability, preventing drastic shifts in behavior.4. Memory Optimization: Since training large language models can be memory-intensive, we apply optimizations such as batch size limits, clearing CUDA cache, and reducing sequence lengths to enable training on resource-constrained hardware.While the full implementation can be found in the sample notebook, we will walk through key parts of the code to better understand the reasoning behind specific design and implementation choices. The core aspects of the training loop are shown in the following snippet.

for epoch in range(NUM_EPOCHS):
    for batch_idx, batch in enumerate(ppo_trainer.dataloader):
        if batch_idx >= 30:  # Limit batches for demo
            break
        query_tensors = batch["input_ids"]
        response_tensors = []
        for i, query in enumerate(query_tensors):
            generation_kwargs["attention_mask"] = batch["attention_mask"][i].unsqueeze(0)
            response = ppo_trainer.generate(query, **generation_kwargs)
            response_tensors.append(response.squeeze()[len(query):])  # Remove prompt tokens
        batch["response"] = [tokenizer.decode(r, skip_special_tokens=True) for r in response_tensors]
        texts = [p + " " + r for p, r in zip(batch["prompt"], batch["response"])]
        rewards_tensor = compute_reward(texts)
        reward_history.append(rewards_tensor.mean().item() if isinstance(rewards_tensor, torch.Tensor) else float(rewards_tensor))
        stats = ppo_trainer.step(query_tensors, response_tensors, rewards_tensor)
        kl_div = stats.get('ppo/policy/approxkl') or stats.get('ppo/policy/policykl') or stats.get('objective/kl')
        kl_divs.append(kl_div)
        if batch_idx % 20 == 0:
            print(f"Batch {batch_idx} Reward: {reward_history[-1]:.4f} KL: {kl_div:.4f}")

The training loop iterates over NUM_EPOCHS and ppo_trainer.dataloader, which provides batches of data during training. Each batch contains batch["input_ids"], structured as tensors and assigned as query_tensors. At this point, the batch does not yet include model-generated responses—response generation is deferred until inside the training loop to optimize memory usage.Each batch also includes attention_masks, stored within the RLHFDataset, which are appended to the generation_kwargs and passed per query when calling ppo_trainer.generate(query, **generation_kwargs) to produce model responses. These responses are collected into response_tensors, which, along with query_tensors, are inputs required for the PPO update.Reward computation uses the compute_reward method, which takes text inputs constructed by combining the decoded batch prompts and generated responses. After obtaining rewards, the PPO step is performed via ppo_trainer.step(query_tensors, response_tensors, rewards) to optimize the policy. As seen in the core training loop, the model generates responses per query, computes corresponding rewards using the compute_reward() method, and applies PPO updates through ppo_trainer.step().After each update, in this case, you can do memory management by clearing memory using clear_memory().Through this iterative RLHF process, the policy model gradually improves its responses based on the rewards it receives. Early outputs may not align well with human preferences, but successive PPO updates encourage the model to optimize toward preferred, coherent, and diverse responses. The KL-divergence penalty acts as a regularizer, ensuring updates are smooth and the model does not deviate drastically from its original behavior.

Expected improvement in response can look something like this:

Before Fine-Tuning:

Prompt: "How should I respond to a rude customer?"

Model Output: "Just ignore them. If they keep bothering you, be rude back."

After Fine-Tuning with PPO Updates:

Prompt: "How should I respond to a rude customer?"

Model Output: "Stay professional and polite. If necessary, calmly explain company policies."

Metrics to Track

Fine-tuning language models using PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization) in the RLHF framework involves several dynamic components. Tracking the right metrics is essential not only for debugging and convergence monitoring but also for ensuring that the model is aligning with human preferences without diverging too far from its base capabilities. Below are key metrics that should be monitored throughout PPO training:

KL Divergence

KL divergence monitors how much the fine-tuned policy deviates from the original reference model. It is one of the most important signals in PPO-based RLHF. It reflects how far the policy has moved from the reference distribution—typically the base, pretrained model. Excessively high KL divergence suggests that the model is becoming overly specialized or overfitted for the reward function, potentially losing generality or linguistic fluency. On the other hand, very low or negative KL values might suggest under-training, poor learning signal, or even issues in numerical stability. In practice, KL divergence is often regularized in the PPO loss function using a coefficient (kl_coef), which may need to be adjusted during training.

Reward Score

The reward score measures the quality of generated responses according to the learned reward model. The reward model plays a central role in RLHF by quantifying how aligned a model’s output is with human preferences. During PPO training, each new response is scored by this model, and its average reward provides insight into how much the agent is improving. A rising reward trend generally reflects alignment progress, assuming the reward model is accurate and calibrated. However, consistently high rewards without a matching improvement in human-perceived quality may indicate reward hacking—where the model optimizes for spurious patterns in the reward model rather than genuine alignment.

Entropy

Entropy indicates how diverse or deterministic the model’s output distribution is. It measures the randomness of the model's policy—the higher the entropy, the more exploratory the behavior. In language generation, this translates to producing a wider range of responses. Maintaining healthy entropy during training helps prevent premature convergence to overly narrow or repetitive outputs. Entropy typically decreases as training progresses, since the policy becomes more confident. However, maintaining moderate entropy is important to preserve diversity and prevent repetitive or generic outputs. If entropy drops too quickly, the model may become too deterministic, reducing creativity and potentially overfitting to the training distribution.

Episode Length or Token Count

The episode length is the number of tokens generated per sample during rollouts. Monitoring the length of generated responses (measured in tokens) can help detect unintended changes in model behavior. For example, if the fine-tuned model starts producing much shorter or longer outputs than expected, this may reflect instability in sampling behavior or flawed reward conditioning. It may also indicate shifts in how the model interprets stopping conditions or special tokens, particularly if special stop tokens are involved in training.

Loss Values

The loss values includes policy loss, value loss, and total PPO loss, which together indicate learning dynamics. While RLHF focuses on human-aligned objectives, the underlying PPO optimization is still gradient-driven. Monitoring loss values helps assess whether the training process is stable. The policy loss reflects how much the model is being updated in response to the advantage estimates. The value loss indicates how well the critic (value function) is predicting future rewards. Sudden spikes or collapses in these values may require adjusting learning rates, clipping coefficients, or reward normalization strategies.In the TRL framework, after each training step using ppo_trainer.step(), a dictionary of metrics called stats is returned. This stats dictionary contains important indicators such as KL divergence (e.g., under keys like 'ppo/policy/approxkl' or 'ppo/policy/policykl'), policy loss, value loss, entropy, and the average reward. These metrics can be logged, printed, or stored for analysis, making it easier to monitor training dynamics and intervene if necessary. By examining the stats output at each iteration, you gain visibility into how well PPO training is progressing and whether any hyperparameters (such as the KL coefficient or learning rate) may need adjustment.Together, these metrics serve as diagnostic tools to ensure PPO training proceeds smoothly. By observing reward trends, KL divergence, policy loss, and entropy, you can make informed adjustments during training, avoid instability, and better interpret the outcome of fine-tuning.

Evaluating the Fine-Tuned Model

After RLHF fine-tuning, the trained model can be saved and evaluated on real-world prompts. To evaluate improvements, we compare responses from the original (pre-trained) model with those of the fine-tuned model. The quality of responses is measured using a reward model, which assigns higher scores to responses aligned with human preferences. This process allows us to assess whether RLHF successfully improved the model’s behavior.After fine-tuning, the model can be evaluated by switching it to evaluation mode using the eval method. Test using some input_text , tokenize it using the tokenizer function. Note that you may get a warning if the attention mask and the pad token ID are not explicitly provided. You need to explicitly provide attention_mask along with the input IDs during inference. Additionally, you should set pad_token_id explicitly for text generation. The generate method is used for for both the fine-tuned model and the reference model. A sample snippet of how you can generate model response is shown as follows:

    with torch.no_grad():
        model.eval()
        ft_output = model.generate(
            input_ids,
            max_new_tokens=MAX_NEW_TOKENS,
            do_sample=True,
            top_p=0.9
        )

Once the output response for model and reference model is generated, we can reuse the compute_reward function to evaluate the improvement in reward for generated output form fine-tuned model relative to the reference model that was unchanged.

# Calculate scores
    original_score = compute_reward([prompt + " " + ref_response])[0].item()
    finetuned_score = compute_reward([prompt + " " + ft_response])[0].item()
    print(f"Original reward: {original_score:.4f}")
    print(f"Fine-tuned reward: {finetuned_score:.4f}")

Note that the fine-tuning using reward models is typical part of the RLHF process, and several experimental training aspects such as generation_kwargs, model configuration parameters, and training parameters must be updated depending upon size of model, dataset training size, token size and reward model. Here we used a pretrained reward model, which was sufficient for demonstration purposes. In practice, a pretrained reward model can be fine-tuned, before using it for RLHF based alignment. In this chapter, we present a starting point for fine-tuning models for alignment. For production cases, more training iterations might be necessary, and some experimentation with training parameters might become essential. Sometimes during training, KL Divergence might become negative, and this should be avoided, as this value should usually range between 0 and 10. There are some training debugging practices and advice discussed on the TRL documentation, https://huggingface.co/docs/trl/en/how_to_train , which can be beneficial. If you are interested in developing production level RLHF policy optimization and model alignment, you can scale the approach to larger base models e.g. gpt3.5 instead of gpt2, or use smaller models but train through more iterations and monitor reward improvements.

Summary

In this chapter, we discussed how to integrate a reward model into the RLHF pipeline and use it to fine-tune a base language model. We revisited PPO algorithm for policy optimization, and discussed how PPO can be modified for RLHF. Then, we looked at TRL library and how preparing datasets, configuring and setting model and training parameters help in integrating pretrained language models, pretrained reward models cand the dataset for training for fine-tuning the model. We also saw how a reward computation function can help use reward model signals during training and act as an evaluation metric for evaluating progress in fine-tuning to align to human preferences. Human Feedback collection is expensive, and we have used pretrained reward models to help alleviate some efforts. But, in practice, a reward collection mechanism has to be set up for custom feedback from human annotators. We will discuss various methods of overcoming the challenges of acquiring human feedback in the next section.
